Chapter 1

General Introduction

India extends from 8°4'N to 37°6'N latitude and 68°7'E to 97°25'E longitude, covering 32,87,263 sq. km (2.4% of world land surface, 7th largest). High-yield exam facts include the Tropic of Cancer traversing 8 states, Indian Standard Time (82°30'E) passing near Shankargarh/Prayagraj in UP, and India's 15,106.7 km land borders.

Geographical Location, Latitudinal Extent & Extreme Points

  • India lies entirely in the Northern and Eastern Hemispheres, extending latitudinally from 8°4' N to 37°6' N and longitudinally from 68°7' E to 97°25' E.
  • The mainland of India spans 3,214 km from North to South (Indira Col in Ladakh to Cape Comorin in Kanyakumari) and 2,933 km from West to East (Guhar Moti in Gujarat to Kibithu in Arunachal Pradesh).
  • India covers an area of 32,87,263 sq. km (ISFR: 32,87,469 sq. km), accounting for approximately 2.4% of the world's total land area while sustaining 17.5% of the world's population (Census 2011).
  • India is the seventh-largest country in the world by land area, after Russia, Canada, USA, China, Brazil, and Australia.
  • Indira Point [इंदिरा पॉइंट] (formerly Pygmalion Point or Parsons Point) at 6°45' N latitude on Great Nicobar Island is India's southernmost geographical point; it was partially submerged during the 2004 tsunami.
  • Indira Col in Ladakh marks India's northernmost point; Kibithu in Anjaw district of Arunachal Pradesh marks the easternmost point; and Guhar Moti (Kutch, Gujarat) marks the westernmost point.
  • Area-wise, India (3.28 million sq. km) is the seventh largest country in the world. India's total area accounts for about 2.4 percent of the total geographical area of the world. Lying entirely in the Northern Hemisphere... UPPSC
  • India is the seventh largest country after Russia, Canada, USA, China, Brazil and Australia respectively by area and the first largest (state of the World Population report, 2023) by population. It occupies about 2.4 per... UPPSC
  • India experiences the first sunrise in Arunachal Pradesh. It is the 7 th largest country in the world in terms of area and the second most populous country globally. According to the state of the World Population report,... UPPSC
  • The state of Gujarat is situated on the west coast of India between 68º 4' East and 74º 4' East longitudes and Arunachal Pradesh is located between 91° 30' East and 97° 30' East longitudes. Thus, the longitudinal differe... UPPSC

Tropic of Cancer & Indian Standard Time (IST) Meridian

  • कर्क रेखा [Tropic of Cancer] (23°30' N latitude) दर्रे [Mountain Passes] through the middle of India, dividing the country into almost equal northern subtropical and southern tropical zones.
  • कर्क रेखा [Tropic of Cancer] दर्रे [Mountain Passes] through 8 Indian states from west to east: Gujarat, Rajasthan, Madhya Pradesh, Chhattisgarh, Jharkhand, West Bengal, Tripura, and Mizoram.
  • Ranchi (capital of Jharkhand) is situated closest to the कर्क रेखा [Tropic of Cancer], while other nearby major state capitals include Gandhinagar and Bhopal.
  • Mahi River in Western India is the only river in India that cuts across the कर्क रेखा [Tropic of Cancer] twice (first in Madhya Pradesh, then in Gujarat).
  • भारतीय मानक समय [Indian मानक समय [Standard Time]] (IST) is based on the 82°30' E longitude meridian, which दर्रे [Mountain Passes] through 5 states: Uttar Pradesh, Madhya Pradesh, Chhattisgarh, Odisha, and Andhra Pradesh.
  • The 82°30' E meridian दर्रे [Mountain Passes] near Shankargarh and Naini in Prayagraj (formerly Allahabad) and Mirzapur district of Uttar Pradesh; IST is 5 hours 30 minutes ahead of Greenwich Mean Time (GMT +5:30).
  • The longitudinal longitudinal difference of nearly 30° between Arunachal Pradesh (east) and Gujarat (west) causes a local sun-time difference of approximately 2 hours (120 minutes).
  • The कर्क रेखा [Tropic of Cancer] दर्रे [Mountain Passes] almost through the middle of India. It passes through 8 Indian states, namely Mizoram, Tripura, West Bengal, Jharkhand, Chhattisgarh, Madhya Pradesh, Rajasthan and Gujarat. Thus, at present no... UPPSC
  • The कर्क रेखा [Tropic of Cancer] दर्रे [Mountain Passes] through a total of eight states in India, which include Gujarat, Rajasthan, Madhya Pradesh, Chhattisgarh, Jharkhand, West Bengal, Tripura, and Mizoram. Before 2000 AD Tropic of Cancer passed t... UPPSC
  • The place whose latitudinal position is at a minimum distance from the latitude of the कर्क रेखा [Tropic of Cancer] will be the nearest place to the Tropic of Cancer. The Tropic of Cancer is at 23° 30' N; divides India into almost t... UPPSC ×3

International Land Frontiers, Boundaries & Coastal Borderlands

  • India shares 15,106.7 km of international land borders with 7 countries: Bangladesh (4,096.7 km - longest), China (3,488 km), Pakistan (3,323 km), Nepal (1,751 km), Myanmar (1,643 km), Bhutan (699 km), and Afghanistan (106 km in PoK - shortest).
  • The international boundary between India and Pakistan is known as the Radcliffe Line [रेडक्लिफ रेखा], drawn in August 1947 by Sir Cyril Radcliffe; Radcliffe line also demarcates the border with Bangladesh.
  • The McMahon Line [मैकमोहन रेखा] was drawn in 1914 at the Simla Convention to demarcate the boundary between British India and Tibet (Arunachal Pradesh sector of India-China border).
  • The Durand Line [डूरंड रेखा] was demarcated in 1893 by Sir Mortimer Durand between British India and Afghanistan; currently it separates Pakistan and Afghanistan, with a 106 km stretch bordering India in Ladakh (PoK).
  • India's total coastline measures 7,516.6 km (mainland coastline: 5,422.6 km, island territories: 2,094 km), touching 9 coastal states: Gujarat (longest coastline ~1,214.7 km), Andhra Pradesh, Tamil Nadu, Maharashtra, Kerala, Odisha, Karnataka, West Bengal, and Goa.
  • Uttar Pradesh shares borders with 8 states (Uttarakhand, Himachal Pradesh, Haryana, Rajasthan, Madhya Pradesh, Chhattisgarh, Jharkhand, Bihar) and 1 Union Territory (Delhi), the highest for any state in India.
  • West Bengal, Assam, Meghalaya, Mizoram and Tripura are the Indian states that share their borders with Bangladesh. Mymensingh, Rangpur, Khulna, Sylhet, Rajshahi and Chittagong are the six divisions of Bangladesh that are... UPPSC ×3
  • Arunachal Pradesh, Nagaland, Manipur and Mizoram are Indian states that share a border with Myanmar but Assam does not share its border with Myanmar. It shares a border with Bangladesh & Bhutan. UPPSC
  • The boundary between India and Pakistan (and India- Bangladesh) was demarcated by Radcliffe Line. l Durand Line is between India and Afghanistan (and Pakistan-Afghanistan). l McMahon Line is between India and China. l Ma... UPPSC
  • India has natural as well as man-made boundaries. The himalayas in the north, Bay of Bengal in the south-east, Indian Ocean in the south and Arabian Sea in the south-west form its natural boundary. India shares its borde... UPPSC

Chapter 2

Physical Division

India's physical divisions encompass the Northern Himalayan Wall, the Peninsular Plateau (oldest stable Gondwana block), Great Northern Plains, Coastal Plains, and Island Archipelagos. Key exam themes cover Himalayan transverse zones, strategic mountain passes, Peninsular peaks (Anamudi, Doddabetta), and Ten Degree Channel.

UPPSC asks most here

  2. Lipulekh Pass is located on India- China border in Uttarakhand not in Uttar Pradesh.PCS Pre ’01; PCS Mains ’142 papers
  3. Anaimudi is the highest peak of Southern India and a part of the Western Ghats mountains, having an elevation of 2,695 metres. It is located...PCS Pre ’05; UDA/LDA Pre ’132 papers

Northern Mountain Region: Himalayas, Glaciers & Mountain Passes

  • The Himalayas are young fold mountains formed during the Tertiary period by the collision of the Indian tectonic plate with the Eurasian plate, compressing sediments of the Tethys Geosyncline.
  • From north to south, the Himalayas are divided into four longitudinal belts: Trans-Himalaya (Tibetan Himalayas - Karakoram, Ladakh, Zaskar), Greater Himalaya / हिमाद्रि [Himadri], Lesser / Middle Himalaya / हिमाचल [Himachal], and Outer Himalaya / शिवालिक [Shiwalik].
  • K2 / Godwin Austen (8,611 m) in the Karakoram Range (PoK) is the highest peak in the Indian subcontinent (2nd highest globally); Kanchenjunga (8,586 m) in Sikkim is the highest peak situated in undisputed Indian territory.
  • Crucial पर्वतीय दर्रे [Mountain Passes] tested by UPPSC: Zoji La (Ladakh - connects Srinagar to Leh), Banihal (J&K - Jawahar Tunnel), Shipki La (HP - Satluj enters India), Rohtang (HP - Atal Tunnel), Lipulekh, Mana and Niti (Uttarakhand - Kailash-Mansarovar route), Nathu La and Jelep La (Sikkim - Indo-China trade), and Bomdi La (Arunachal Pradesh).
  • Siachen Glacier [सियाचिन हिमनद] (76 km long) in the Nubra Valley of Karakoram is the world's longest glacier outside polar regions and the highest battlefield on Earth; other key हिमनद [Glaciers] include Baltoro, Biafo, Hispar, and Gangotri.
  • Karewa formations [करेवा] in the Kashmir Valley are thick lacustrine silt deposits of the Pleistocene period, world-renowned for the cultivation of GI-tagged Zafran (Saffron).
  • Map Not to Scale The Deccan Traps were formed in Western Central India during the Cretaceous period of the Mesozoic era, covering a large part of Maharashtra. V olcanic fissure eruptions led to lava flows that covered ap... UPPSC
  • The Western coastline of India is not formed by the deposition process of the rivers. In fact, it is formed by the submergence and emergence of the land . Gondwana rocks have the largest reserve of कोयला [Coal] in India. The Him... UPPSC
  • The rivers originating from the Himalayas are perennial because their source is located in the Himalayan हिमनद [Glaciers]. The Himalaya receives much of its rain from the south-western monsoon. Assertion and Reason both are true... UPPSC
  • In the Himalayan region (Jammu & Kashmir, Ladakh, Himachal Pradesh, Uttarakhand, etc.), rivers are fed by snow/हिमनद [Glaciers] as well as rainfall, so they are perennial. The steep gradient, narrow valleys, and landforms like w... UPPSC

Peninsular Plateau, Mountain Ranges & Highest Peaks

  • प्रायद्वीपीय पठार [Peninsular Plateau] is an ancient, rigid, triangular shield composed of Pre-Cambrian crystalline and metamorphic rocks, constituting the oldest geological landmass of India (part of Gondwanaland).
  • Aravalli Range [अरावली श्रेणी] is the oldest fold mountain system in India and one of the oldest in the world (Pre-Cambrian era, now residual mountains); its highest peak is Guru Shikhar (1,722 m) at Mount Abu in Rajasthan.
  • Western Ghats / सह्याद्रि [Sahyadri] form a continuous escarpment from Tapti valley to Kanyakumari; pierced by four major दर्रे [Mountain Passes]: Thal Ghat (Mumbai-Nashik), Bhor Ghat (Mumbai-Pune), Pal Ghat (Palakkad Gap - Kochi-Coimbatore), and Shencottah Gap.
  • Western Ghats and Eastern Ghats converge at the Nilgiri Hills [नीलगिरि पहाड़ियाँ] ('Blue Mountains'); Doddabetta (2,637 m) is the highest peak of the Nilgiris.
  • Anamudi [अनाईमुडी] (2,695 m) in the Anaimalai Hills of Kerala is the highest mountain peak in South India and the entire Peninsular region.
  • Vindhya and Satpura ranges run parallel across central India; Dhupgarh (1,350 m) near Pachmarhi on Mahadeo Hills is the highest peak of the Satpura Range.
  • Shillong Plateau / Meghalaya Plateau is a detached eastern extension of the प्रायद्वीपीय पठार [Peninsular Plateau] (not part of the Himalayas), separated from the Rajmahal Hills by the Malda Gap (Garo-Rajmahal Gap).
  • Kuttanad (or Kuttanadu) of Kerala is famous for its lowest altitude in India. Kuttanad is called the Rice Bowl of Kerala. Here farming is practiced (4 to 10 ft.) 1.2 to 3.0 meters below sea level. FAO has declared the Ku... UPPSC
  • The rocks of the Dharwar order were formed by the transformation or faulting of the rocks of the Archean order. These rocks are highly metallic, in which minerals like gold, Iron, Manganese, अभ्रक [Mica], Cobalt, Chromium, Coppe... UPPSC
  • Aksai Chin is spread over about 38000 square kilometers in Ladakh region. It is an Indian territory under the occupation of China. UPPSC

Great Northern Plains, Coastal Belts & Island Groups

  • The Great Plains of North India are formed by the deep aggradational deposition of alluvium brought by the Indus, Ganga, and Brahmaputra river systems into a massive foredeep depression.
  • The plains feature three distinct longitudinal belts: भाबर [Bhabhar] (8-16 km wide pebble/gravel belt along the Shiwalik foot where streams disappear), तराई [Terai] (marshy, humid, densely forested belt south of Bhabhar where streams re-emerge), and the vast जलोढ़ मैदान [Alluvial Plain].
  • The जलोढ़ [Alluvial] plains comprise older elevated alluvium containing calcareous kankar nodules called बांगर [Bangar], and younger, highly fertile floodplains renewed annually called खादर [Khadar].
  • Western coastal plain is narrow and emergent, subdivided into Konkan Coast (Maharashtra/Goa), Canara/Karnataka Coast, and Malabar Coast (Kerala - famous for Kayals/lagoons like Vembanad).
  • Eastern coastal plain is broad and deltaic, subdivided into the Coromandel Coast (Tamil Nadu/AP) and Northern Circars (Odisha/WB), housing Chilika Lake (largest brackish water lagoon in India) and Pulicat Lake.
  • Ten Degree Channel [10° चैनल] (10° N latitude) separates the Andaman Islands (Little Andaman) from the Nicobar Islands (Car Nicobar) in the Bay of Bengal.
  • Barren Island [बैरन द्वीप] in the Andaman Sea is India's and South Asia's only active volcano; Narcondam Island is a dormant volcanic island.
  • Lakshadweep [लक्षद्वीप] in the Arabian Sea comprises 36 low-lying atoll islands of biogenic coral origin; Nine Degree Channel separates Minicoy Island from the main Lakshadweep archipelago; Eight Degree Channel separates Minicoy from Maldives.

Chapter 4

Species/Tribes

India recognizes over 700 Scheduled Tribes (STs), with Bhils and Gonds comprising the largest tribal populations under Census 2011. UPPSC questions heavily test UP tribes (Tharu, Buksa, Kol, Kharwar), the Toda buffalo pastoralists of Nilgiri, and Andaman PVTGs.

Major Tribes of India, Regional Distribution & Cultural Practices

  • According to Census 2011, Scheduled Tribes constitute 8.6% of India's total population; Bhils are the largest tribal group in India (over 1.7 crore), followed closely by Gonds and Santhals.
  • Toda tribe [टोडा जनजाति] inhabits the Nilgiri Hills of Tamil Nadu; they are traditional pastoralists centered around sacred water buffalo herds, speak a Dravidian language, and historically practiced fraternal polyandry.
  • Jarawa, Sentinelese, Onge, and Great Andamanese are indigenous hunter-gatherer tribes of Negrito stock inhabiting the Andaman Islands, classified as Particularly Vulnerable Tribal Groups (PVTGs).
  • Garo, Khasi, and Jaintia tribes of Meghalaya follow a traditional matrilineal descent and matrilocal residence system.
  • Lepcha and Bhutia tribes inhabit Sikkim and the Darjeeling hills of West Bengal; Bodo and Miri tribes are indigenous to Assam; Chenchu tribe inhabits the Nallamala forests of Andhra Pradesh and Telangana.
  • Santhal tribe predominantly inhabits Jharkhand, West Bengal, and Odisha; famous for the historic Santhal Hool (Rebellion) of 1855 led by Sidhu and Kanhu.

Scheduled Tribes of Uttar Pradesh & Central India

  • Tharu tribe [थारू जनजाति] is the most populous Scheduled Tribe in Uttar Pradesh, residing along the moist तराई [Terai] belt in Maharajganj, Siddharthnagar, Shravasti, Bahraich, and Lakhimpur Kheri districts.
  • The Tharu tribe is uniquely known for celebrating the Hindu festival of Diwali as a day of mourning / sorrow [शोक पर्व] to commemorate their departed ancestors; they follow a joint family system and claim Kirat lineage.
  • Buksa / Bhoksa tribe [बुक्सा जनजाति] resides primarily in Bijnor district of Uttar Pradesh; they claim descent from the historic Patwar Rajput clan of Marwar and practice monogamy.
  • Sonbhadra district possesses the highest tribal population (both absolute number and percentage ~20.7%) among all districts in Uttar Pradesh, housing Agariya, Baiga, Chero, Gond, and Kharwar communities.
  • Baghpat district has the lowest Scheduled Tribe population in Uttar Pradesh, with almost negligible tribal presence.
  • Sahariya tribe [सहरिया] is concentrated primarily in Lalitpur district of Uttar Pradesh and neighboring Madhya Pradesh; Kol tribe is found widely in Prayagraj, Mirzapur, and Banda districts.

← Chapter 3Languages of India →

Chapter 5

Languages of India

Indian languages belong to four major families: Indo-Aryan (~78%), Dravidian (~20%), Austro-Asiatic (Munda), and Sino-Tibetan. The Eighth Schedule recognizes 22 official languages, with Hindi and Bengali being the most widely spoken, while UP recognizes Hindi and Urdu.

Linguistic Families of India & Major Language Groups

  • The Indo-Aryan language family [भारतीय-आर्य भाषा परिवार] is the largest linguistic group in India, spoken by approximately 78% of the population, comprising Hindi, Bengali, Marathi, Gujarati, Punjabi, Sindhi, Odia, Assamese, and Sanskrit.
  • The Dravidian language family [द्रविड़ भाषा परिवार] is the second largest group (~20% speakers), comprising the major literary languages Telugu, Tamil, Kannada, and Malayalam, along with tribal languages like Gondi, Kurukh, and Kui.
  • The Austro-Asiatic (Munda) family [ऑस्ट्रो-एशियाटिक भाषा परिवार] is spoken primarily by indigenous tribes of central and eastern India, including Santhali, Mundari, Ho, Bhumij, and Khasi in Meghalaya.
  • Hindi is the most widely spoken language in India (~43.6% native speakers as per Census 2011), followed by Bengali (8.03%), Marathi (6.86%), Telugu (6.7%), and Tamil (5.7%).
  • According to Ethnologue 2025, Bengali ranks as the 7th most spoken language in the world (~284 million global users) and the second most spoken in the Indian subcontinent after Hindi.

Scheduled Languages, Classical Status & UP Official Languages

  • The Eighth Schedule of the Indian Constitution originally listed 14 regional languages in 1950, which has expanded to 22 recognized scheduled languages through three constitutional amendments.
  • Sindhi was added to the Eighth Schedule by the 21st Constitutional Amendment Act (1967); Konkani, Manipuri, and Nepali were added by the 71st Amendment (1992); Bodo, Dogri, Maithili, and Santhali were added by the 92nd Amendment (2003).
  • The Government of India confers Classical Language status [शास्त्रीय भाषा] based on high antiquity of early texts (1500-2000 years); Tamil was declared first in 2004, followed by Sanskrit (2005), Telugu and Kannada (2008), Malayalam (2013), and Odia (2014).
  • In October 2024, five additional languages were granted Classical status: Marathi, Pali, Prakrit, Assamese, and Bengali, bringing the total to 11 Classical Languages.
  • Uttar Pradesh has two officially recognized state languages: Hindi was declared the first official language in 1947, and Urdu was enacted as the second official language through the UP Official Language (Amendment) Act 1989.
← Chapter 4Drainage System →

Chapter 6

Drainage System

India's drainage systems are divided into perennial Himalayan rivers (Ganga, Indus, Brahmaputra) and rain-fed Peninsular rivers (Godavari, Krishna, Narmada, Kaveri). UPPSC focuses on Ganga's Panch Prayag confluences, rift-valley flowing Narmada-Tapi, and UP rivers (Yamuna, Betwa, Ken, Son, Gomti).

  1. Major rivers of Peninsular India originate from the Western Ghats. Eastward flowing peninsular rivers follow the long route and also form De...PCS Pre ’14; UDA/LDA Pre ’10; RO/ARO Mains ’133 papers
  2. Damodar is a tributary of river – → Hooghly.UDA/LDA Pre ’06; Lower Sub (Spl) ’08; PCS Pre ’083 papers
  3. Sutlej (Satluj) river rises from Rakas Lake in Tibet. Flowing northwest, it enters Himachal Pradesh near Shipki La. It creates deep gorges c...RO/ARO Mains ’14; PCS Mains ’122 papers

Himalayan Drainage System: Ganga, Indus & Brahmaputra Basins

  • The Himalayan अपवाह तंत्र [Drainage System] comprises perennial antecedent rivers originating in trans-Himalayan हिमनद [Glaciers]: the Indus, the Ganga, and the Brahmaputra, cutting dramatic deep gorges across the mountain wall.
  • Ganga River (2,525 km - India's longest river) originates as the Bhagirathi from Gangotri हिमनद [Glacier] at Gaumukh (Uttarkashi); at Devprayag [देवप्रयाग], Bhagirathi merges with Alaknanda to form the Ganga.
  • Panch Prayag [पंच प्रयाग] along the Alaknanda in downstream order: Vishnuprayag (Alaknanda + Dhauliganga), Nandprayag (Alaknanda + Nandakini), Karnaprayag (Alaknanda + Pindar), Rudraprayag (Alaknanda + Mandakini), and Devprayag (Alaknanda + Bhagirathi).
  • Major left-bank tributaries of Ganga include Ramganga, Gomti, Ghaghara, Gandak, Kosi (known as 'Sorrow of Bihar' for catastrophic course shifts), and Mahananda.
  • Indus River (Sindhu, 2,880 km total, 1,114 km in India) rises near Mansarovar Lake in Tibet; key tributaries form the Panjnad: Jhelum (originates Verinag, flows through Wular Lake), Chenab (Chandra-Bhaga confluence, largest tributary), Ravi, Beas, and Satluj (rises at Rakshastal near Mansarovar).
  • Brahmaputra rises from Chemayungdung/Angsi हिमनद [Glacier] in Tibet as the Yarlung Tsangpo; cuts around Namcha Barwa entering Arunachal Pradesh as Siang/Dihang; forms Majuli (world's largest river island) in Assam; enters Bangladesh as the Jamuna and merges with the Padma (Ganga).
  • The Ganga river is an example of antecedent drainage. UPPSC
  • After entering Bangladesh, the main branch of the Ganges is known as 'Padma'. The Padma is joined by the Brahmaputra river (known as Jamuna). Further, it meets Meghna river and is commonly known as Meghna, which flows in... UPPSC

Peninsular River Systems: East-Flowing vs West-Flowing Drainage

  • The Peninsular अपवाह तंत्र [Drainage System] is older than the Himalayas, characterized by broad, mature, shallow valleys and rain-fed, graded, non-perennial river courses.
  • Godavari River (1,465 km) is the longest peninsular river in India, originating at Trimbakeshwar (Nashik, Maharashtra); known as 'Dakshin Ganga' [दक्षिण गंगा] or 'Vriddha Ganga'; major tributaries: Penganga, Wainganga, Wardha, Pranhita, Indravati, and Manjira.
  • Krishna River (1,400 km) rises at Mahabaleshwar in Maharashtra; major tributaries include Tungabhadra, Koyna, Bhima, Ghataprabha, Malaprabha, and Musi (Hyderabad is on Musi).
  • Kaveri River (800 km) originates at Talakaveri in the Brahmagiri Hills (Kodagu, Karnataka); flows through Karnataka and Tamil Nadu; unlike other peninsular rivers, Kaveri receives rain in both summer (SW monsoon) and winter (NE monsoon), remaining semi-perennial.
  • Narmada (1,312 km) and Tapti (724 km) flow westward into the Arabian Sea through structural rift valleys [भ्रंश घाटी] between the Vindhya and Satpura ranges; they do NOT form deltas, forming deep tidal estuaries [ज्वारनदमुख] instead.
  • Narmada originates at Amarkantak plateau in Madhya Pradesh, creating the picturesque Dhuandhar Falls [धुआंधार जलप्रपात] on marble rocks near Jabalpur; Tapti originates at Multai in Betul district (MP).
  • Mahanadi (851 km) rises in the highlands of Sihawa (Dhamtari, Chhattisgarh) and flows through Odisha, where Hirakud Dam is constructed across it.
  • "Moribund Delta" is a sub-division of Bengal Delta. The Moribund delta areas are in the southwestern part of the Bangladesh, south of the Ganges river, where tributaries are highly silted and are profusion of oxbow lakes... UPPSC
  • Shimoga, Ballari and Chikkamagaluru are Manganese pro- ducing areas of Karnataka, while Srikakulam is a district in Andhra Pradesh, which is mainly known for the production of quartz, feldspar and other ferro-magnesium m... UPPSC
  • The Tibetan Plateau is also known in China as the Qinghai- Tibet Plateau or the Qing Zang. With an average elevation exceeding 4,500 meters, the Tibetan Plateau is also called "the Roof of the World". The rivers originat... UPPSC

Drainage of Uttar Pradesh, Waterfalls & Important Lakes

  • Ganga River enters Uttar Pradesh at Bijnor district and exits at Ballia district, traversing 28 districts and covering its longest course of ~1,140 km within the state.
  • Yamuna River (1,376 km) is Ganga's longest right-bank tributary, originating at Yamunotri हिमनद [Glacier] (Bandarpunch peak); enters UP at Saharanpur, flows past Mathura, Agra, and Etawah, meeting Ganga at the holy Sangam in Prayagraj.
  • Gomti River [गोमती नदी] is unique as the only major Ganga tributary originating entirely in the plains, emerging from Fulhar Jheel / Gomat Taal in Pilibhit district (UP); flows through Lucknow, Sultanpur, and Jaunpur, joining Ganga at Kaithi (Ghazipur).
  • Chambal River rises at Janapav hills near Mhow (MP), flows through Rajasthan and UP, meeting Yamuna in Etawah; notorious for carving extensive badland ravines [बीहड़ / खड्ड]; major dams: Gandhi Sagar (MP), Rana Pratap Sagar, and Jawahar Sagar (Rajasthan).
  • Betwa River rises in Raisen (MP), traverses Lalitpur, Jhansi, and Hamirpur, joining Yamuna at Hamirpur; Ken River rises in Kaimur hills and meets Yamuna at Chilla in Banda district; Son River rises at Amarkantak, traverses Sonbhadra district of UP, and joins Ganga near Patna.
  • Prominent waterfalls and lakes tested by UPPSC: Chitrakote Falls on Indravati river in Bastar (known as the 'Niagara of India'), Jog/Gersoppa Falls on Sharavathi (Karnataka), Nohkalikai Falls (Meghalaya); in UP: Keetham/Sur Sarovar (Agra - Ramsar site), Bakhira Lake (Sant Kabir Nagar - Ramsar site), and Govind Ballabh Pant Sagar (Sonbhadra).
  • The Gomti river originates from Gomat Taal (Fulhar lake) near Pilibhit district in Uttar Pradesh. It is the only tributary of River Ganges which rises in the plains. UPPSC
  • The Ram Ganga river originates from Dudhatoli ranges in Pauri Garhwal, Uttarakhand. It is a tributary of the river Ganges while Chambal, Betwa and Ken are tributaries of river Yamuna. UPPSC

  • River Betwa rises in the Vindhya Range and it is a tributary of Yamuna. It joins the river Yamuna near Hamirpur town Uttar Pradesh. UPPSC
← Chapter 5Climate →

Chapter 7

Climate

India features a tropical monsoon climate governed by the South-West Monsoon (supplying ~75% of annual rainfall) and the winter North-East Monsoon. UPPSC frequently tests Western Disturbances causing winter rains in Western UP and extreme rainfall variations across UP (Gorakhpur highest, Mathura lowest).

UPPSC asks most here

  1. Which one of the following places has the lowest annual rainfall ? → Leh.PCS Mains ’10; PCS Pre ’082 papers
  2. Local Storm Main Region Norwesters / Kal-Baisakhi West Bengal, Assam and Northeast India Blossom Shower Kerala–Karnataka (South India) Mango...RO/ARO Pre ’231 paper
  3. The Tamil Nadu coast does remain dry during the South- West monsoon season. This is because it is situated parallel to the Bay of Bengal bra...PCS Pre ’231 paper

Mechanism of Indian Monsoon & Rainfall Distribution

  • India's climate is classified as Tropical Monsoon [उष्णकटिबंधीय मानसूनी जलवायु], characterized by seasonal reversal of wind direction driven by differential heating of land and sea and shift of the Inter-Tropical Convergence Zone (ITCZ).
  • दक्षिण-पश्चिम मानसून [South-West Monsoon] (June to September) advances across India in two main branches: the Arabian Sea Branch and the Bay of Bengal Branch, supplying over 75% of India's total annual precipitation.
  • Mawsynram and Cherrapunji on the southern slopes of Khasi Hills in Meghalaya receive the highest average annual rainfall in the world (>1,100 cm) due to the funneling relief of the Meghalaya plateau.
  • The windward western slopes of the Western Ghats receive heavy orographic rainfall exceeding 250 cm, while the leeward Deccan plateau lies in the rain-shadow zone [वृष्टि छाया क्षेत्र] receiving under 60 cm.
  • Global climatic oscillations significantly influence monsoon performance: El Niño generally suppresses Indian monsoon rainfall, whereas La Niña and positive Indian Ocean Dipole (IOD) phases promote abundant monsoon rains.

  • Kochi and Tejpur experience more humid climate due to heavy rainfall. In 2022, Kochi (Ernakulam) experienced rainfall of up to 3415.8mm, Ahmedabad- 564.0 mm, Ludhiana- 766.9mm and Tejpur – 1775.7 mm (Sonitpur). Compared ... UPPSC
  • Tamil Nadu, located in south-east peninsular India, receives the major part of its annual rainfall during the उत्तर-पूर्वी मानसून [North-East Monsoon] season. Tamil Nadu receives about 40-50% of its annual rainfall during North-East monsoon. UPPSC

Western Disturbances, Winter Rainfall & UP Climate Dynamics

  • पश्चिमी विक्षोभ [Western Disturbances] are extra-tropical temperate cyclonic storms originating over the Mediterranean Sea, steered into northwest India by the subtropical westerly jet stream during winter months (December–February).
  • पश्चिमी विक्षोभ [Western Disturbances] bring light to moderate winter rainfall to Punjab, Haryana, Delhi, and Western Uttar Pradesh, which is immensely beneficial for the रबी [Rabi] wheat crop, while causing heavy snowfall in the Western Himalayas.
  • उत्तर-पूर्वी मानसून / लौटता मानसून [Retreating / उत्तर-पूर्वी मानसून [North-East Monsoon]] (October to December) picks up moisture over the Bay of Bengal and yields heavy winter rainfall along the Coromandel Coast of Tamil Nadu.
  • Within Uttar Pradesh, Gorakhpur district receives the highest annual rainfall (averaging ~184 cm), while Mathura district receives the lowest annual rainfall (averaging ~54 cm).
  • Uttar Pradesh experiences three distinct seasons: Hot Dry Summer (March to June, marked by blistering dusty western winds called 'Loo'), Rainy Season (June to October, driven by the Bay of Bengal monsoon branch entering from the east), and Cold Winter Season (November to February).
  • Mansoon originates by which of the following winds in India? → South-W est winds. UPPSC

← Chapter 6Natural Disasters →

Chapter 8

Natural Disasters

India's physiography predisposes it to geological earthquakes (Himalayan Zone V), coastal tropical cyclones (Bay of Bengal), riverine floods (Eastern UP), and arid droughts (Bundelkhand). UPPSC tests seismic classification zones, cyclone naming systems, and disaster management under the NDMA Act 2005.

UPPSC asks most here

  2. The western disturbance is responsible for the winter rains in the northwest part of India, but the western disturbance is a weak form of cy...PCS Pre ’211 paper
  3. Cyclone "Tauktae" originated in the Arabian Sea. This storm hit the coast of Gujarat in May 2021.RO/ARO Pre ’211 paper

Seismic Vulnerability Zones, Earthquakes & Tsunami Risk

  • The Bureau of Indian Standards (BIS) categorizes India into four Seismic Zones: Zone V (Very High Damage Risk - Zone factor 0.36), Zone IV (High Damage Risk - 0.24), Zone III (Moderate Damage Risk - 0.16), and Zone II (Low Damage Risk - 0.10); Zone I was eliminated.
  • Zone V encompasses the entire North-Eastern states, parts of Jammu & Kashmir, Ladakh, Himachal Pradesh, Uttarakhand, the Rann of Kutch (Gujarat), and the Andaman and Nicobar Islands.
  • Most of the Gangetic plain in Uttar Pradesh falls under Seismic Zone III (Moderate Risk), while northern foothill districts bordering Nepal (e.g. Saharanpur, Muzaffarnagar, Bijnor) lie in Seismic Zone IV.
  • The catastrophic Indian Ocean Tsunami of 26 December 2004 was triggered by a 9.1 magnitude undersea megathrust earthquake off the west coast of northern Sumatra, devastating the Coromandel Coast of Tamil Nadu and the Andaman & Nicobar Islands.
  • National Disaster Management Authority [राष्ट्रीय आपदा प्रबंधन प्राधिकरण] (NDMA) was constituted under the Disaster Management Act 2005, functioning under the Ministry of Home Affairs and ex-officio chaired by the Prime Minister of India.

Tropical Cyclones, Floods & Droughts in Eastern UP & Bundelkhand

  • चक्रवात [Tropical चक्रवात [Cyclones]] develop over the North Indian Ocean during pre-monsoon (April–May) and post-monsoon (October–November) periods; the Bay of Bengal witnesses four times more cyclones than the Arabian Sea due to warmer surface temperatures and upper air easterlies.
  • चक्रवात [Cyclone] naming in the North Indian Ocean is managed by the WMO/ESCAP panel comprising 13 member countries, with names assigned sequentially from pre-approved national lists.
  • Eastern Uttar Pradesh (districts like Gorakhpur, Deoria, Kushinagar, Basti, Siddharthnagar, Balrampur) experiences severe recurrent floods caused by the silt-choked channels of the Ghaghara, Rapti, Gandak, and Rohini rivers.
  • Bundelkhand region [बुंदेलखंड] spanning 7 southern UP districts (Jhansi, Lalitpur, Jalaun, Hamirpur, Mahoba, Banda, Chitrakoot) is chronically afflicted by agricultural drought due to erratic monsoon rainfall, poor soil moisture capacity, and hard rocky terrain.
  • National Institute of Disaster Management (NIDM) conducts premium research and capacity building in disaster resilience, headquartered in New Delhi.

Chapter 9

Soils

ICAR classifies Indian soils into 8 orders, with Alluvial (~40%), Black / Regur (~15%), Red (~18%), and Laterite being the most significant. UPPSC tests self-ploughing black cotton soils, Gangetic Khadar vs Bangar, sub-Himalayan Bhabhar and Terai, and Bundelkhand soils (Rakar, Parwa, Mar, Kabar).

  2. Laterite has been derived from the Latin word ‘Later’ which means brick. The laterite soils develop in areas with high temperature and high ...PCS Pre ’231 paper
  3. In India, saline soils are found to have an excess of Sodium and Potassium a high Magnesium ratio and deficiency of lime, hence this soil is...RO/ARO Pre ’231 paper

Major Soil Groups of India: Alluvial, Black, Red & Laterite

  • The Indian Council of Agricultural Research (ICAR) classifies Indian soils into 8 major categories, among which जलोढ़ मिट्टी [Alluvial Soil], काली मिट्टी [Black Soil], लाल मिट्टी [Red Soil], and लैटेराइट मिट्टी [Laterite Soil] are commercially paramount.
  • जलोढ़ मिट्टी [Alluvial Soil] is India's most widespread and productive soil group (~40% area), formed by river deposition in the Indo-Gangetic-Brahmaputra plains and coastal deltas; rich in potash and lime, but deficient in nitrogen, phosphorus, and organic humus.
  • काली मिट्टी [Black Soil / रेगुर [Regur]] (काली मिट्टी [Black Cotton Soil]) is derived from the weathering of Deccan basaltic lava (Maharashtra, Gujarat, western MP); dominated by montmorillonite clay, high moisture retention, swells when wet, and develops deep cracks when dry, conferring a 'self-ploughing' [स्वतः जुताई] attribute ideal for dryland cotton cultivation.
  • लाल मिट्टी [Red Soil] develops on crystalline igneous Archean rocks under low rainfall; its red hue is caused by wide diffusion of iron oxides (ferric oxide); highly porous, poor in nitrogen, phosphorus, and humus, requiring irrigation for crops.
  • लैटेराइट मिट्टी [Laterite Soil] forms under high temperatures and alternating intense wet and dry spells through severe leaching [निक्षालन]; rich in iron oxide and aluminium residues, but acidic and poor in lime, silica, and potash; ideal for रोपण फसलें [Plantation Crops] (tea, coffee, rubber, cashew).
  • 'Ockhi' is a cyclonic storm that hit India's coast in November 2017. This चक्रवात [Cyclone] was categorized as an 'extremely severe cyclonic storm.' UPPSC
  • रेगुर [Regur] Soils are formed by the weathering and erosional work of Basaltic lava. The Regur soil is also known as काली मिट्टी [Black Soil], काली मिट्टी [Black Cotton Soil] or the Tropical Black Earth or Tropical Chernozem. UPPSC ×2
  • काली मिट्टी [Black Soil] is also known as रेगुर [Regur] soil. It is a mature soil and has high water retention capacity. Self-ploughing is a characteristic of black soil as it develops wide cracks when dried. UPPSC
  • The काली मिट्टी [Black Soil] of the Deccan Trap is also known as रेगुर [Regur] or Reguda. The black soil is very suitable for cotton cultivation. It lacks in phosphorus, nitrogen and organic matter (humus). The black soil is rich in Alumina, ... UPPSC
  • 'काली मिट्टी [Black Soil]' or 'रेगुर [Regur] Soil' or 'काली मिट्टी [Black Cotton Soil]' is generally clayey, deep and impermeable. It swells and becomes sticky when wet, and shrinks when dried. So, during the dry season, this soil develops wide cracks. Thu... UPPSC

Soils of Uttar Pradesh: Bhabhar, Terai, Alluvium & Bundelkhand Formations

  • In Uttar Pradesh, the northern sub-Himalayan belt is divided into: भाबर [Bhabhar] (a narrow gravelly boulder tract where rivers sink underground) and तराई [Terai] (a damp, swampy, silt-rich जलोढ़ [Alluvial] zone south of Bhabhar, highly fertile for sugarcane and paddy).
  • The Gangetic plain of UP comprises two broad जलोढ़ [Alluvial] types: खादर [Khadar] (newer, light-colored floodplains renewed annually by flood silts, highly fertile) and बांगर [Bangar] (older upland alluvium characterized by calcareous nodules called कंकड़ [Kankar], less fertile).
  • Bundelkhand plateau soils [बुंदेलखंड की मिट्टियाँ] in southern UP are locally divided into: Rakar [राकर] (red gravelly soil on slopes), Parwa [पड़वा] (yellow-grey sandy loam), Mar [मार] (fertile black clay soil), and Kabar [काबर] (sticky dark काली मिट्टी [Black Soil]).
  • Saline and Alkaline soils (locally designated as ऊसर [Usar], रेह [Reh], or कल्लर [Kallar]) are widespread in semi-arid canal-irrigated districts of central UP (Mainpuri, Aligarh, Etah, Kanpur, Raebareli, Unnao); reclaimed by application of gypsum and pyrite.
  • Severe soil erosion occurs in the Chambal and Yamuna basins (Agra, Etawah, Jalaun districts) due to intense gully erosion [अवनलिका अपरदन], forming deep badland ravines [बीहड़].
  • Zinc is the micronutrient that is most deficient in Indian soils among the given options. UPPSC

  • The diameter of clay is less than 0.002 mm. Silt has a diameter of 0.002 mm to 0.05 mm and fine sand's diameter is 0.05 mm to 2 mm. UPPSC

Chapter 10

Natural Vegetation

Tropical Moist Deciduous forests form India's most extensive forest biome (~37%), dominated by Teak and Sal. UPPSC questions test ISFR forest cover statistics (MP largest area, Mizoram highest percentage), Dudhwa National Park, and UP's State tree/flower symbols.

Forest Classification of India & ISFR Data

  • The National Forest Policy 1988 envisions maintaining at least 33% of India's total geographical area under forest and tree cover (60% in hilly/mountainous zones and 20% in the plains) to preserve ecological balance.
  • Tropical Moist Deciduous forest [उष्णकटिबंधीय आर्द्र पर्णपाती वन] is India's most widespread forest type (covering ~37% of total forest area), flourishing in 100-200 cm rainfall zones; Sal (Shorea robusta) and Teak (Tectona grandis) are the primary commercial timber species.
  • Tropical Dry Deciduous forests occur in 70-100 cm rainfall regions, shedding leaves during spring/early summer; common species include Tendu, Palas, Amaltas, Bel, Khair, and Axlewood.
  • Tropical Wet Evergreen forests thrive in high-rainfall zones (>250 cm) of the Western Ghats, Northeast India, and Andaman & Nicobar; featuring Rosewood, Ebony, Mahogany, and Aini.
  • According to the India State of Forest Report (ISFR): Madhya Pradesh possesses the largest forest cover by area, while Mizoram possesses the highest forest cover as a percentage of geographical area (~84.5%).
  • मैंग्रोव वन [Mangrove Forests] cover littoral and tidal swamp coasts; Sundarbans in West Bengal is the largest tidal halophytic mangrove forest in the world, dominated by Sundari trees (Heritiera fomes).
  • The destruction of soil cover is described as soil erosion. The soil forming processes and the erosional processes of running water and wind go on simultaneously. But generally, there is a balance between these two proce... UPPSC
  • Sal (Shorea robusta) is not of the evergreen forest type. It is generally a prominent moist deciduous tree, whereas Deodar, Silver Fir, and Blue Pine are evergreen (conifers) trees. UPPSC
  • The tree line is the edge of the habitat at which trees are capable of growing. The value of the natural tree-line in the Eastern Himalayas and Central Himalayas is about 3600- 3800m. In the western and northwestern Hima... UPPSC
  • The Cinchona is an evergreen tropical plant. In India, evergreen forests are found in Tamil Nadu, Karnataka, Kerala, Maharashtra, Assam, Arunachal Pradesh, Meghalaya, Nagaland, Tripura, West Bengal and theAndaman and Nic... UPPSC

Forest Cover, Wildlife Sanctuaries & National Parks of Uttar Pradesh

  • Uttar Pradesh has a relatively low forest cover of approximately 6.15% (forest + tree cover ~9.23%); Sonbhadra district boasts the highest forest cover (both in total area and percentage ~35%), followed by Chandauli and Chitrakoot.
  • Bhadohi (Sant Ravidas Nagar) district has the lowest forest cover in Uttar Pradesh (under 0.35%).
  • पलास / ढाक [Palas / Dhak] (Butea monosperma), popularly known as the 'Flame of the Forest' [जंगल की आग], is the official State Flower of Uttar Pradesh.
  • Official State symbols of Uttar Pradesh: State Tree is Ashoka (Saraca asoca); State Animal is Barasingha / Swamp Deer (Rucervus duvaucelii); State Bird is Sarus Crane (Grus antigone).
  • Dudhwa राष्ट्रीय उद्यान [National Park] [दुधवा राष्ट्रीय उद्यान] in Lakhimpur Kheri district is the only National Park in Uttar Pradesh, established in 1977 and famous for Swamp Deer and Royal Bengal Tigers.
  • Four designated Tiger Reserves in Uttar Pradesh: Dudhwa Tiger Reserve, Pilibhit Tiger Reserve (won TX2 award), Amangarh Tiger Reserve (buffer zone of Corbett in Bijnor), and Ranipur Tiger Reserve in Chitrakoot (notified as UP's 4th and India's 53rd Tiger Reserve).
  • Chandraprabha वन्यजीव अभयारण्य [Wildlife Sanctuary] [चंद्रप्रभा वन्यजीव अभयारण्य] in Chandauli district (established 1957) is the oldest wildlife sanctuary in UP, once chosen for the reintroduction of Asiatic lions.

Chapter 11

Irrigation and Canals

Tube-wells and wells constitute ~62% of India's net irrigated area, followed by Canals (~24%) and Tanks (~3%). UPPSC emphasizes UP leading India in net irrigated area and tube-well density, the historic Sharda Canal network, and the landmark Ken-Betwa river interlinking project.

Sources of Irrigation in India: Tube-wells, Canals & Tanks

  • Tube-wells and open wells constitute the largest source of irrigation in India (~62%), followed by government and private Canals (~24%), Tanks (~3%), and other sources.
  • Uttar Pradesh ranks #1 in India in net irrigated area and #1 in tubewell-irrigated area, with tube-wells irrigating approximately 80% of the state's net cultivated land.
  • Tanks [तालाब] are the dominant traditional irrigation source in Peninsular South India (Tamil Nadu, Andhra Pradesh, Telangana) due to the undulating crystalline rocky terrain, which prevents canal excavation and promotes rainwater harvesting in natural depressions.
  • Canal irrigation is most extensively developed in the northern जलोढ़ [Alluvial] plains of Punjab, Haryana, and Uttar Pradesh due to perennial snow-fed rivers and soft, easily excavated alluvial soils.
  • Micro-irrigation techniques (Drip and Sprinkler irrigation under Pradhan Mantri Krishi Sinchayee Yojana - PMKSY) are heavily promoted for water-use efficiency in water-stressed arid regions.

  • The development of tubewell irrigation has been most spectacular in the Saryupar Plain, due to lack of canals. Hence irrigation in this area is done by tubewells. Thus, the correct answer is option (c). UPPSC

Canal Systems of Uttar Pradesh & Inter-Basin River Links

  • शारदा नहर [Sharda Canal] is the longest and most extensive canal network in Uttar Pradesh (completed in 1928), originating from the Banbasa Barrage on the Sharda River (Kali river) on the India-Nepal border.
  • ऊपरी गंगा नहर [Upper Ganga Canal] was engineered by Sir Proby Cautley and inaugurated in 1854; it originates from Bhimoda Barrage near Haridwar, irrigating the western Doab region of UP.
  • निचली गंगा नहर [Lower Ganga Canal] was constructed in 1878, originating from the Narora Barrage on the Ganga in Bulandshahr district, irrigating central UP plains.
  • Agra Canal originates from the Okhla Barrage on the Yamuna river in Delhi, irrigating parts of Haryana, Delhi, Rajasthan, and Agra/Mathura in Uttar Pradesh.
  • Betwa Canal originates from the Parichha Barrage on the Betwa river in Jhansi district, providing vital irrigation to the drought-prone Bundelkhand districts of Jhansi, Jalaun, and Hamirpur.
  • The landmark Ken-Betwa Link Project [केन-बेतवा नदी जोड़ो परियोजना] is India's first national river interlinking project under the National Perspective Plan, building the Daudhan Dam to transfer surplus water from Ken river (MP) to Betwa basin (UP), irrigating over 2.5 lakh hectares in Bundelkhand.
  • The Lower Ganga Canal draws its water from the Ganga river at Narora (Bulandshahr). It was completed in 1878. UPPSC
  • The 'Hariyali' scheme was launched by the then Prime Minister Atal Bihari Vajpayee on 27 January, 2003 under watershed management project. This project is being run through 2.32 lakh panchayats of the country. This proje... UPPSC

Chapter 12

Multi-Purpose River Valley Project

Multipurpose river valley projects serve irrigation, flood protection, and hydroelectric generation. UPPSC tests DVC (India's first corporation, 1948), Bhakra-Nangal, Hirakud, Tehri, Sardar Sarovar, and UP's iconic Rihand Dam (Govind Ballabh Pant Sagar) and Mata Tila Dam.

Iconic Multipurpose River Valley Projects of India

  • Prime Minister Jawaharlal Nehru hailed multi-purpose river valley projects as the 'Temples of Modern India' [आधुनिक भारत के मंदिर] for their foundational role in industrial and agricultural self-reliance.
  • Damodar Valley Corporation [दामोदर घाटी निगम] (DVC), established in July 1948, was independent India's first multipurpose river valley project, modeled directly after the Tennessee Valley Authority (TVA) of the USA; major dams include Tilaiya, Maithon, and Panchet.
  • Bhakra-Nangal Project on the Satluj River is a joint venture of Punjab, Haryana, and Rajasthan; Bhakra Dam is one of the highest concrete straight gravity dams in the world (226 m), creating the Gobind Sagar reservoir in Himachal Pradesh.
  • Tehri Dam [टिहरी बांध] on the Bhagirathi River (at confluence with Bhilangana) in Tehri Garhwal (Uttarakhand) is the highest earth-and-rockfill dam in India (260.5 m height).
  • Hirakud Dam [हीराकुंड बांध] on the Mahanadi River in Sambalpur district of Odisha is the longest earthen dam in the world (~25.8 km total length with dykes).
  • Sardar Sarovar Dam on the Narmada River in Navagam, Gujarat, is a massive concrete gravity dam serving Gujarat, Madhya Pradesh, Maharashtra, and Rajasthan; opposed by the Narmada Bachao Andolan led by Medha Patkar.
  • Other key national dams: Nagarjuna Sagar on Krishna River (Andhra Pradesh/Telangana), Tungabhadra on Tungabhadra River (Karnataka/AP), and Idukki Dam on Periyar River in Kerala (double-curvature concrete arch dam).
  • Sardar Sarovar Dam (height - 163 meters) has been constructed on Narmada river near Navagam in Gujarat. The beneficiary States of the Sardar Sarovar Project are Gujarat, Rajasthan, Maharashtra and Madhya Pradesh. Hence (... UPPSC ×2

  • Harsud town of district Khandwa in Madhya Pradesh was submerged in 2004 due to the construction of Indira Sagar Dam. UPPSC

River Valley Projects, Dams & Reservoirs in Uttar Pradesh

  • Rihand Dam [रिहंद बांध] is constructed across the Rihand River (a tributary of the Son) at Pipri in Sonbhadra district of Uttar Pradesh; its reservoir, Govind Ballabh Pant Sagar [गोविंद वल्लभ पंत सागर], is India's largest man-made artificial lake by volume.
  • Rihand Dam's जल विद्युत [Hydroelectric Power] plant supplies dedicated, cheap electrical power to the Hindalco aluminium smelting plant at Renukoot in Sonbhadra.
  • Mata Tila Dam [माताटीला बांध] (also known as Rani Laxmibai Dam) is constructed across the Betwa River in Lalitpur district of Uttar Pradesh; a joint interstate project of UP and Madhya Pradesh completed in 1958.
  • Rajghat Dam [राजघाट बांध] is another major multipurpose dam on the Betwa River in Lalitpur district, executed jointly by the governments of Uttar Pradesh and Madhya Pradesh.
  • Parichha Dam [पारीछा बांध] is situated on the Betwa River near Jhansi, acting as a crucial reservoir for the Parichha ताप विद्युत [Thermal Power] Station.
  • Ban Sagar Dam [बाणसागर बांध] is built across the Son River in Shahdol district of Madhya Pradesh as a tri-state joint venture between MP, UP, and Bihar (sharing costs and benefits in 2:1:1 ratio), providing canal irrigation to Mirzapur and Prayagraj districts.
  • The oldest hydropower plant is in Darjeeling district in West Bengal. It was commissioned in the year 1897. The जल विद्युत [Hydroelectric Power] station near Shivasamudram was set up on the Kaveri River in Karnataka. It is the second... UPPSC

Chapter 13

Agriculture

Agriculture sustains over 45% of India's workforce under 15 Planning Commission Agro-Climatic Zones (and 9 distinct zones in UP). UPPSC emphasizes marginal/small holdings (<2 ha, ~86%), cropping seasons (Kharif, Rabi, Zaid), and UP's undisputed national agricultural primacy.

Agro-Climatic Zones of India & Nine Zones of Uttar Pradesh

  • The Planning Commission classified India into 15 broad Agro-Climatic Zones based on physiography, soil profile, geological formation, climate, and water resources.
  • Uttar Pradesh is subdivided into 9 distinct Agro-Climatic Zones [कृषि जलवायु क्षेत्र] for agricultural planning: 1. Tarai Region, 2. Western Plain, 3. Central Western Plain, 4. South Western Semi-Arid, 5. Central Plain, 6. Bundelkhand Region, 7. North Eastern Plain, 8. Eastern Plain, and 9. Vindhyan Region.
  • The Tarai region of UP receives high rainfall and has rich alluvium, favoring sugarcane and paddy; the Bundelkhand and Vindhyan zones suffer from moisture stress, specializing in दलहन [Pulses], तिलहन [Oilseeds], and millets.
  • The Western Plain and Central Western Plain zones in UP have near-100% assured irrigation, representing the most technologically advanced agricultural belt in the state.

  • Govind Vallabh Pant University of Agriculture and Technology is the first agricultural university of India. It was inaugurated by Jawaharlal Nehru on 17 November 1960 as the “Uttar Pradesh Agricultural University” (UPAU)... UPPSC

Cropping Seasons (Kharif, Rabi, Zaid) & Operational Landholdings

  • India features three distinct cropping seasons: खरीफ [Kharif] (June–July to Sept–Oct, monsoon crops: Rice, Maize, Jowar, Bajra, Cotton, Jute, Arhar), रबी [Rabi] (October–December to March–April, winter crops: Wheat, Barley, Gram, Mustard, Peas), and जायद [Zaid] (short summer season March–June: Watermelon, Muskmelon, Cucumber, Vegetables).
  • Operational landholdings in India are categorized into 5 classes: Marginal (<1 hectare), Small (1–2 hectares), Semi-Medium (2–4 hectares), Medium (4–10 hectares), and Large (>10 hectares).
  • According to Agricultural Census data, Marginal and Small farmers constitute approximately 86% of all operational agricultural holdings in India, reflecting acute land fragmentation.
  • Net Sown Area constitutes roughly 43-45% of India's total geographical area, with the cropping intensity hovering around 140-145%.
  • Uttar Pradesh ranks #1 in India in total foodgrain production, contributing nearly 18-19% of national foodgrain output.
  • 'The History of Indian Agriculture' is written by M.S. Randhawa. His full name is Mohinder Singh Randhawa. He played a major role in the field of agriculture research and हरित क्रांति [Green Revolution] in India. UPPSC

Chapter 14

Green Revolution

The First Green Revolution (1966-67) achieved food self-sufficiency through semi-dwarf HYV wheat seeds, chemical fertilizers, and tubewell irrigation. UPPSC tests Dr. Borlaug, Dr. Swaminathan, Pantnagar University, Western UP's lead, and Evergreen/Rainbow revolutions.

  3. The Blue Revolution refers to the remarkable emergence of aquaculture. Aquaculture refers to all form of active culturing of aquatic animals...PCS Mains ’161 paper

Genesis, Inputs & High Yielding Varieties of Green Revolution

  • हरित क्रांति [Green Revolution] in India was launched in 1966-67 (during the Annual Plans period) to overcome severe food deficits and end reliance on US PL-480 food imports.
  • Dr. Norman Ernest Borlaug (USA/CIMMYT Mexico) is recognized as the 'Father of the हरित क्रांति [Green Revolution] in the World'; he received the Nobel Peace Prize in 1970 for breeding high-yielding semi-dwarf wheat varieties using Norin-10 dwarfing genes.
  • Dr. M.S. Swaminathan [डॉ. एम.एस. स्वामीनाथन] is revered as the 'Father of the हरित क्रांति [Green Revolution] in India', spearheading the adaptation and field deployment of Mexican semi-dwarf varieties (Lerma Rojo 64A and Sonora 64).
  • Govind Ballabh Pant University of Agriculture & Technology (Pantnagar, established in 1960 in erstwhile UP, now Uttarakhand) served as the pioneering cradle of the हरित क्रांति [Green Revolution] in India.
  • The technological package of the हरित क्रांति [Green Revolution] relied on the synergistic 'seed-fertilizer-water technology': High-Yielding Variety (HYV) seeds, assured tubewell/canal irrigation, chemical fertilizers (ideal NPK ratio 4:2:1), and plant protection pesticides.
  • The term 'Evergreen Revolution' was used for increasing agricultural production in India. It was used by Father of India's हरित क्रांति [Green Revolution], Dr. M.S. Swaminathan. He described 'Evergreen Revolution' as increasing product... UPPSC
  • Norman Ernest Borlaug was born on 25 March 1914 in Cresco, Iowa, U.S.A. He took up an agricultural research position in Mexico, where he developed semi-dwarf, high- yield, disease-resistant wheat varieties. During the mi... UPPSC
  • The हरित क्रांति [Green Revolution] in India was started in 1966-67 which led to an increase in food grain production. The main development was higher yielding varieties of wheat which increased the production to 2.5 times. Its credit ... UPPSC
  • Wheat is the highest beneficiary of the हरित क्रांति [Green Revolution] in both production and productivity followed by rice. UPPSC ×2
  • As a result of the हरित क्रांति [Green Revolution], the yield per hectare of wheat touched a record figure of 2000 Kg (three times as earlier). 2024-25 Yield of wheat crops is 3595 kg/hectare. UPPSC

Crop-Wise Impact, Western UP Lead & Agricultural Revolutions

  • The हरित क्रांति [Green Revolution] disproportionately benefited Wheat [गेहूं] production (often termed a 'Wheat Revolution'), followed secondarily by Rice; coarse cereals (millets) and दलहन [Pulses] experienced stagnant or negative relative growth.
  • Geographically, the First हरित क्रांति [Green Revolution] was heavily concentrated in Punjab, Haryana, and Western Uttar Pradesh (Meerut, Muzaffarnagar, Bulandshahr, Aligarh) due to preexisting canal infrastructure and progressive farming.
  • Dr. M.S. Swaminathan later advocated for an 'Evergreen Revolution' [सदाबहार क्रांति], emphasizing productivity improvement in perpetuity without causing ecological or social harm.
  • Rainbow Revolution [इंद्रधनुषी क्रांति] encompasses the integrated development of all agricultural sectors: Green (foodgrains), White (milk / Operation Flood), Yellow (तिलहन [Oilseeds]), Blue (fisheries), Golden (fruits/horticulture/honey), Grey (fertilizers), Silver (eggs/poultry), and Round (potato).
  • हरित क्रांति [Green Revolution] started in the 7th decade in the year 1966-67; which was its first stage. In the first stage, Haryana, Punjab and Western Uttar Pradesh were included. UPPSC
  • The correct match between list-I and list-II is as follows: Growth in food Productions - हरित क्रांति [Green Revolution] Milk Production - White Revolution Fisheries - Blue Revolution Fertilizers - Grey Revolution UPPSC
  • Pink Revolution is associated with Onion. Other important Revolutions are as follows – Black Revolution - Petroleum. Red Revolution - Tomato and Meat Blue Revolution - Fisheries Yellow Revolution - तिलहन [Oilseed] UPPSC
  • The Blue Revolution refers to the remarkable emergence of aquaculture. Aquaculture refers to all form of active culturing of aquatic animals, plants and fishes. It is not related to poultry. Thus, it is not correctly mat... UPPSC

Food Crops

Rice (Kharif) and Wheat (Rabi) are India's twin staples, complemented by coarse millets (Shree Anna). UPPSC frequently examines varieties (IR-8, Jaya, Kalyan Sona, Sonalika), fungal diseases like Karnal Bunt, and UP ranking #1 in wheat and total foodgrains.

Wheat & Rice: Cultivation Requirements, Varieties & Leading States

  • Rice [चावल] is the foremost staple food of India, occupying the largest cropped area; a tropical खरीफ [Kharif] crop requiring high temperatures (20°-27°C) and abundant rainfall (>100 cm); West Bengal is the leading producer, followed by Uttar Pradesh (2nd) and Punjab (3rd).
  • Punjab leads India in per-hectare productivity of both rice and wheat due to near-100% tubewell irrigation and intensive fertilizer application.
  • Jaya was bred in India and acclaimed as the 'Miracle Rice' [चमत्कारी चावल], outyielding international semi-dwarf variety IR-8; other notable varieties include Ratna, Padma, Mahsuri, and Pusa Basmati 1121.
  • Wheat [गेहूं] is India's premier रबी [Rabi] food crop, requiring cool growing weather (15°-20°C at sowing) and bright warm sunshine at ripening, with 50-75 cm rainfall; Uttar Pradesh ranks #1 in India in both acreage and total production of wheat.
  • High-yielding wheat varieties repeatedly tested by UPPSC: Kalyan Sona, Sonalika, Sharbati Sonora, PBW-343, HD-2967, and Raj-3077.
  • Karnal Bunt [करनाल बंट] is a serious fungal disease affecting wheat crops caused by the fungus Tilletia indica, first detected at Karnal (Haryana) in 1931.
  • Blue Revolution in India is related to Pisciculture. UPPSC
  • Blue Revolution is related to fisheries not poultry. Thus option (d) is not correctly matched. UPPSC

Coarse Cereals, Millets (Shree Anna) & UP Foodgrain Dominance

  • Millets / मोटे अनाज [Coarse Cereals], branded as 'Shree Anna' [श्री अन्न], are highly climate-resilient, drought-hardy, nutrient-rich crops requiring minimal water; the United Nations declared 2023 as the International Year of Millets on India's proposal.
  • Bajra [बाजरा] (Pearl Millet) is the most widely cultivated millet in India, thriving in arid sandy soils; Rajasthan is the undisputed leading producer, followed by Uttar Pradesh.
  • Jowar [ज्वार] (Sorghum) is the third most important food crop in India in area and production, cultivated as both खरीफ [Kharif] and रबी [Rabi]; Maharashtra is the leading producer, followed by Karnataka.
  • Maize [मक्का] is a versatile crop grown for food, fodder, and industrial starch; requires 21°-27°C; Karnataka and Madhya Pradesh are the leading producers; hybrid varieties include Ganga-1, Deccan, and Sartaj.
  • Uttar Pradesh ranks #1 among all Indian states in total foodgrain production [कुल खाद्यान्न उत्पादन], generating nearly 18-19% of national foodgrain output.

Sugarcane: Growing Belts, Processing & UP Sugar Dominance

  • Sugarcane [गन्ना] is India's premier commercial cash crop, a tropical and subtropical crop requiring 21°-27°C temperature and 75-100 cm annual rainfall, thriving in rich जलोढ़ [Alluvial] and loamy soils.
  • Uttar Pradesh is the undisputed leading producer of Sugarcane in India, accounting for roughly 45-50% of the country's total sugarcane acreage and harvest.
  • Western Uttar Pradesh (Meerut, Muzaffarnagar, Saharanpur, Shamli, Baghpat, Bijnor) forms the primary 'Sugarcane Belt' of the state, boasting the highest concentration of sugar mills.
  • Maharashtra and southern peninsular states achieve higher sugar recovery rates and longer factory crushing periods than UP due to maritime humid climate and absence of winter frost.
  • Co. 1148, Co. 0238 (Karan 4), and Co. 7704 are prominent high-yielding varieties of Sugarcane tested in UPPSC examinations.
  • Indian Institute of Sugarcane Research [भारतीय गन्ना अनुसंधान संस्थान] (IISR) is located at Lucknow, while the National Sugar Institute [राष्ट्रीय शर्करा संस्थान] (NSI) is situated at Kanpur in Uttar Pradesh.
  • Cotton is primarily a खरीफ [Kharif] season cash crop. The most suitable temperature for Cotton Production ranges between 210C and 270C. It cannot tolerate temperatures below 15 0C or above 43 0C. Frost is harmful of cotton and ... UPPSC

Cotton & Jute: Black Soil Belt & Golden Fibre Regions

  • Cotton [कपास] ('White Gold') is a tropical/subtropical खरीफ [Kharif] cash crop requiring 210 frost-free days, abundant sunshine, and light rainfall (50-100 cm); thrives best in the deep moisture-retentive काली मिट्टी [Black Cotton Soil / रेगुर [Regur]] of the Deccan Trap.
  • Gujarat is the leading producer of cotton in India, followed closely by Maharashtra and Telangana.
  • Bt-Cotton [बीटी-कपास], genetically modified with the Cry1Ac endotoxin gene from Bacillus thuringiensis to resist destructive bollworm pests, accounts for over 90% of India's cotton acreage.
  • Jute [जूट] ('Golden Fibre' / सुनहरा रेशा) requires hot and humid climate (>27°C, >150 cm rainfall) and fertile river silt renewed annually by floods.
  • West Bengal is the overwhelming leader in Jute production, contributing over 75-80% of India's total raw jute, concentrated in the fertile जलोढ़ [Alluvial] floodplains of the lower Ganga (Hooghly basin).
  • Central Research Institute for Jute and Allied Fibres (CRIJAF) is situated at Barrackpore in West Bengal.
  • काली मिट्टी [Black Soil] is suitable for cotton cultivation which covers the maximum area in Maharashtra. Cotton crop is known as White Gold in Maharashtra. UPPSC
  • Cotton fibers are obtained from seeds. UPPSC

Groundnut & Mustard: Varieties, Pegging & Leading Producers

  • Yellow Revolution [पीली क्रांति] was launched in the late 1980s under the Technology Mission on तिलहन [Oilseeds] to attain national self-reliance in edible oil production.
  • Groundnut [मूंगफली] is a premier खरीफ [Kharif] तिलहन [Oilseed] crop, native to South America, thriving in well-drained sandy loams; Gujarat ranks #1 in groundnut production in India, followed by Rajasthan and Tamil Nadu.
  • Pegging [पेगिंग] is a vital reproductive growth stage in groundnut where, following fertilization, the flower stem (peg) elongates downward and penetrates into the soil to develop pods underground.
  • 'Kaushal', 'Chitra', and 'Somnath' are well-known high-yielding varieties of Groundnut.
  • Rapeseed and Mustard [सरसों एवं राई] are the foremost रबी [Rabi] तिलहन [Oilseeds] in India, requiring a cool, dry climate; Rajasthan is the leading producer (~45% of national output), followed by MP and Haryana.
  • 'Varuna' (T-59) [वरुणा], Pusa Bold, Kranti, and Narendra Rai are prominent high-yielding varieties of Mustard tested repeatedly in UPPSC exams.
  • Yellow Revolution is related to the production of तिलहन [Oilseeds] and हरित क्रांति [Green Revolution] is related to the production of foodgrain. White Revolution is related to the production of milk and the Blue Revolution is related to the p... UPPSC ×2

Soybean, Sunflower & Commercial Oilseed Economy

  • Soybean [सोयाबीन] is a dual-purpose miracle legume rich in both high-grade plant protein (~40%) and edible oil (~20%); cultivated as a खरीफ [Kharif] crop.
  • Madhya Pradesh is famously designated as the 'Soybean State' / 'Soya State' [सोया राज्य], leading India in soybean acreage and harvest, followed by Maharashtra.
  • Directorate of Soybean Research (DSR) is situated at Indore in Madhya Pradesh.
  • Sunflower [सूरजमुखी] is a photo-insensitive तिलहन [Oilseed] crop that can be cultivated in खरीफ [Kharif], रबी [Rabi], or जायद [Zaid] seasons; Karnataka is the leading producer in India.
  • Other commercial तिलहन [Oilseeds] include Sesamum (Til - खरीफ [Kharif] in north, रबी [Rabi] in south), Linseed (Alsi - Rabi oilseed), and Castor seed (Gujarat is the leading producer); Uttar Pradesh produces substantial quantities of mustard, sesame, and linseed.

Pulses

India is the world's largest producer, consumer, and importer of pulses, which enrich soil via Rhizobium nitrogen fixation. UPPSC highlights Gram (Rabi, #1 pulse), Arhar/Tur, the Bundelkhand pulse bowl of UP, and IIPR Kanpur.

Major Pulses: Gram, Tur/Arhar & Nitrogen Fixation Traits

  • दलहन [Pulses] constitute the primary dietary source of protein for India's predominantly vegetarian population; leguminous pulse crops harbor symbiotic Rhizobium bacteria in root nodules that fix atmospheric nitrogen, restoring soil fertility (except Arhar which has low fixation).
  • India is simultaneously the world's largest producer, largest consumer, and largest importer of दलहन [Pulses].
  • Gram [चना] (Chickpea) is the single most important pulse crop in India, accounting for nearly 40-45% of total pulse production; a रबी [Rabi] crop cultivated on light loamy and clayey soils; prominent varieties include 'Radhey', 'Pusa-256' (KBG-38), and 'Avarodhi' (wilt-resistant).
  • Arhar / Tur [अरहर/तूर] (Pigeonpea) is the second most important pulse crop, cultivated as a deep-rooted खरीफ [Kharif] crop with a long maturity period (6-9 months); leading producers are Maharashtra and Madhya Pradesh; popular varieties include 'Bahar', 'UPAS-120', and 'Malviya Chamatkar'.
  • Moong (Green gram) and Urad (Black gram) are short-duration दलहन [Pulses] cultivated in both खरीफ [Kharif] and जायद [Zaid] seasons, fitting well into crop rotations.
  • Mustard is an तिलहन [Oilseed]. Pusabold, Jaikisan, Varuna are the main varieties of Mustard. UPPSC
  • Pitambari (RYSK - 05-02) is a variety of Yellow Mustard. Maturity period of Pitambari Mustard is 110-115 days while average production is 1536 kg/per hectare. UPPSC
  • (Crop) (Varieties) Gram – Pant-1 14, Gaurav, Radhe, K-4 Cotton – Suvin, Sujata, Desi Shyamli Groundnut – Chitra-64, Chandra, Kaushal Wheat – Kundan, Kalyan Sona, Sonalika UPPSC
  • The scientific name of 'Safflower' is Carthamus Tinctorius. The Seed contain 24-36% oil. Maharashtra and Karnataka are major safflower growing states. The oil is as good as sunflower oil having a sufficient amount of lin... UPPSC
  • Among the given options, a major producer and consumer of दलहन [Pulses] is India. According to Agricultural Statistics at a Glance 2024, India is the largest producer (in 2022) of Pulses in the world, followed by Canada and Chi... UPPSC

Production Trends, Bundelkhand Pulse Bowl & Kanpur IIPR

  • Madhya Pradesh ranks #1 among all Indian states in total pulse production, followed by Maharashtra and Rajasthan.
  • Indian Institute of दलहन [Pulses] Research [भारतीय दलहन अनुसंधान संस्थान] (IIPR) is located at Kalyanpur, Kanpur in Uttar Pradesh, spearheading national pulse research under ICAR.
  • Bundelkhand region [बुंदेलखंड] spanning Jhansi, Jalaun, Lalitpur, Hamirpur, Mahoba, and Banda acts as the undisputed 'Pulse Bowl' of Uttar Pradesh, specializing in gram, lentil (Masoor), and pigeonpea under rain-fed conditions.
  • National Food Security Mission (NFSM-दलहन [Pulses]) and Accelerated Pulses Production Programme (A3P) have supported high-yield certified seeds and micro-irrigation to reduce import dependence.

  • 'Aparna' is a new high- yielding, leafless variety of pea. UPPSC

Silk

India is the unique global producer of all four commercial silk varieties: Mulberry (~70%), Tasar, Eri, and Muga (golden silk endemic to Assam). UPPSC tests Karnataka's mulberry leadership, Tasar in Jharkhand, and the historic Banarasi silk handloom cluster of UP.

Four Varieties of Commercial Silk & Sericulture in India

  • रेशम कीट पालन [Sericulture] is the agro-based practice of rearing silkworms for the production of raw रेशम [Silk]; India is the second largest producer of silk in the world after China.
  • India possesses the unique global distinction of commercially producing all four known varieties of natural रेशम [Silk]: शहतूती [Mulberry Silk], तसर [Tasar Silk], एरी [Eri Silk], and मूगा [Muga Silk].
  • Mulberry रेशम [Silk] (produced by the domesticated silkworm Bombyx mori feeding on mulberry leaves) accounts for approximately 70-75% of India's total raw silk production.
  • Karnataka is the undisputed leader in Mulberry रेशम [Silk] production, contributing nearly 45-50% of national output, centered in Mysuru, Mandya, Bengaluru Rural, and Kolar districts.
  • Central रेशम [Silk] Board (CSB) was established in 1948 by an Act of Parliament to regulate and promote रेशम कीट पालन [Sericulture], headquartered in Bengaluru (Karnataka).
  • When the question was asked three- fourths of India’s total raw रेशम [Silk] production came from Karnataka and Andhra Pradesh. Presently, 2023-24 these two states jointly account for more than 58.99% of the total raw silk produ... UPPSC

Muga, Tasar, Eri Belts & Varanasi Silk Weaving Cluster

  • Muga रेशम [Silk] [मूगा रेशम] is an exquisite wild golden-yellow silk variety produced by the silkworm Antheraea assamensis, completely endemic to Assam; protected by a Geographical Indication (GI) tag.
  • Tasar रेशम [Silk] [तसर रेशम] is a wild copper-brown silk produced by silkworms fed on forest trees like Asan and Arjun; Jharkhand ranks #1 in Tasar silk production in India, traditionally gathered by tribal communities.
  • Eri रेशम [Silk] [एरी रेशम] is spun from cocoons of silkworms fed on castor leaves, predominantly produced in Assam and Meghalaya; known as 'Ahimsa Silk' because worms emerge naturally without boiling the cocoon.
  • Varanasi (Kashi) in Uttar Pradesh is globally renowned for its traditional handloom Banarasi रेशम [Silk] Sarees [बनारसी रेशमी साड़ी] featuring intricate gold and silver zari brocade work (GI tagged).
  • Other prominent रेशम [Silk] handloom weaving centers in Uttar Pradesh include Mubarakpur in Azamgarh district and Mau.
  • Surya is a variety of Mango, Papaya and Potato. It is not a variety of Linseed. 'रेशम [Silk]' * India is the second largest producer of silk in the world after China. * Mulberry Silk, Tasar Silk, Muga Silk and Eri Silk are four... UPPSC

Chapter 20

Plantation Crops

Plantation crops comprise large-scale commercial monocultures of Tea (Assam #1), Coffee (Karnataka #1), Natural Rubber (Kerala #1), and Spices. UPPSC focuses on ecological criteria (sloping acidic soils for tea, frost-free weather) and southern spice hills.

UPPSC asks most here

  1. Largest pr oducer of coffee in India is – → Karnataka.GIC ’10; Lower Sub ’03; Lower Sub ’023 papers

  3. A state known to be as ‘Spice Garden’ is – (a) Kerala (b ) Karnataka (c) T ami Nadu (d) Gujarat → Option A.RO/ARO Mains ’13; UDA/LDA Mains (Spl) ’102 papers

Tea & Coffee: Ecological Requirements & Producing Regions

  • रोपण फसलें [Plantation Crops] are capital-intensive commercial crops cultivated on large estates for domestic consumption and export.
  • Tea [चाय] (Camellia sinensis) is a shade-loving sub-tropical beverage crop requiring 20°-30°C temperature, heavy rainfall (150-250 cm) well-distributed throughout the year, and deep, friable, well-drained acidic loamy soils on gentle hill slopes (stagnant water rots roots).
  • Assam is the leading producer of tea in India, accounting for over 50% of national output (Brahmaputra and Surma valleys), followed by West Bengal (Dooars and Darjeeling); Darjeeling Tea was awarded India's first Geographical Indication (GI) tag in 2004-05.
  • Coffee [कॉफी] requires warm and humid climate (15°-28°C), 150-200 cm rainfall, and fertile humus-rich soils on hill slopes sheltered from direct blazing sunlight.
  • Karnataka is the undisputed leader in coffee production, contributing roughly 70% of India's coffee, centered in the Western Ghats districts of Kodagu, Chikmagalur, and Hassan.
  • Two commercial coffee species are grown in India: Coffea arabica (superior mild aroma, sensitive to rust) and Coffea robusta (hardy, higher yield, used in instant coffee); Coffee Board is headquartered at Bengaluru.

Rubber, Coconut, Cashew & Southern Spice Plantations

  • Natural Rubber [रबर] is extracted as latex from Hevea brasiliensis, an equatorial tree requiring high temperatures (>25°C) and heavy rainfall (>200 cm); Kerala produces over 75% of India's natural rubber, with the Rubber Board located at Kottayam.
  • Coconut [नारियल] thrives along warm tropical coastlines; Kerala, Karnataka, and Tamil Nadu are the leading producers; Coconut Development Board is headquartered at Kochi.
  • Kerala is traditionally acclaimed as the 'Spice Garden of India' [भारत का मसाला उद्यान], celebrated for Black Pepper ('King of Spices' / 'Black Gold') and Small Cardamom ('Queen of Spices').
  • Cardamom Hills [इलायची पहाड़ियाँ] (Yela Mala) in the southern Western Ghats along the Kerala-Tamil Nadu border are globally renowned for premium cardamom and pepper cultivation.
  • Cashew Nut [काजू] was introduced to India by the Portuguese in Goa; Maharashtra is the leading cashew-producing state, with Kollam in Kerala acting as the world's historic cashew processing hub.
  • National Horticulture Board was established in the year 1984, under Society Registration Act of 1860 as an independent society. Its headquarter is located in Gurugram (Haryana). UPPSC

Chapter 21

Jhuming Agriculture

Shifting cultivation (slash-and-burn / Jhuming) is practiced by tribal communities across hilly forest tracts, causing severe soil erosion and forest loss. UPPSC tests the distinctive local vernacular names of shifting cultivation across different Indian states.

  3. According to 2024-25 data, the three leading cashew nut producers in India are – Maharashtra, Andhra Pradesh and Odisha.UDA/LDA Pre ’131 paper

Shifting Cultivation: Agronomic Practices & Ecological Effects

  • झूम कृषि [Shifting Cultivation] (Slash-and-Burn agriculture / कर्तन एवं दहन प्रणाली) is a primitive subsistence farming system where a patch of forest is cleared by felling and burning vegetation, cultivated for 2-3 years, and then abandoned to fallow for natural regeneration.
  • Ash from burnt trees adds potash to the soil temporarily, but the rapid decline in natural soil fertility and weed infestation forces tribes to shift to a fresh forest plot.
  • Shortening of the fallow cycle (from 15-20 years historically to 2-4 years currently) due to population pressure has caused catastrophic deforestation, accelerated soil erosion, and biodiversity loss.
  • झूम कृषि [Shifting Cultivation] is most prevalent among indigenous tribal communities inhabiting the hill tracts of Northeast India (Assam, Meghalaya, Nagaland, Mizoram, Manipur, and Arunachal Pradesh).
  • According to 2024-25 data, farming of cloves is done in Tamil Nadu, Kerala, Karnataka. Hence the question asked is wrong. UPPSC
  • According to 2024-25 data, the three leading cashew nut producers in India are – Maharashtra, Andhra Pradesh and Odisha. UPPSC

Regional Local Nomenclature of Shifting Cultivation Across States

  • झूम कृषि [Shifting Cultivation] is identified by specific local vernacular names across different Indian states and hill regions, a topic tested with high frequency in UPPSC exams:
  • Jhum [झूम]: North-Eastern states (Assam, Meghalaya, Nagaland, Mizoram, Arunachal Pradesh).
  • Bewar [बेवर] or Dahiya [दहिया]: Madhya Pradesh (practiced traditionally by Baiga and Gond tribes).
  • Podu [पोडू], Dabi [दाबी], Koman [कोमान], or Bringa [बृंगा]: Odisha and Andhra Pradesh.
  • Kumari [कुमारी]: Western Ghats hill tracts in Kerala and Karnataka.
  • Valre [वालरे] or Waltre [वाल्टरे]: South-eastern Rajasthan (practiced by Bhils).
  • Khil [खिल]: Himalayan belt of Himachal Pradesh and Uttarakhand.
  • Kuruwa [कुरुवा]: Santhal Pargana and Chota Nagpur plateau in Jharkhand.
  • Penda [पेंडा]: Bastar district of Chhattisgarh.

Horticulture, Major Fruits & State-Wise Agricultural Ranks

  • बागवानी [Horticulture] encompasses fruits, vegetables, flowers, spices, and रोपण फसलें [Plantation Crops]; promoted by the Golden Revolution [सुनहरी क्रांति] under the National Horticulture Mission (NHM).
  • India ranks #2 in the world in total fruit and vegetable production after China, and #1 globally in the production of Mango, Banana, Papaya, and Okra.
  • Mango [आम] (Mangifera indica) is the National Fruit of India; key varieties include Alphonso (Ratnagiri, Maharashtra), Dasheri (Malihabad, Lucknow, UP), Langra (Varanasi, UP), Chausa and Safeda (Saharanpur, UP), and Banganapalle (Andhra Pradesh).

  • Apple [सेब] cultivation is concentrated in temperate Himalayan valleys of Jammu & Kashmir and Himachal Pradesh; Nashik (Maharashtra) is celebrated as the 'Wine Capital of India' / 'Grape City'.

Specialty Crops, Floriculture & Uttar Pradesh Agro Hubs

Chapter 23

Animal Husbandry

India possesses the world's largest livestock population and ranks #1 in global milk production, catalyzed by Operation Flood under Dr. Verghese Kurien. UPPSC tests Bhadawari buffalo of UP/MP, Jamnapari goats of Etawah, CIRG Mathura, and IVRI Bareilly.

Livestock Census, Operation Flood & Dairy Development

  • पशुपालन [Animal Husbandry] contributes over 30% to the total agricultural Gross Value Added (GVA); according to the 20th पशुधन [Livestock] Census, India possesses the world's largest livestock population (~535.8 million).
  • India ranks #1 in the world in total milk production, contributing approximately 24% of global milk output.
  • Operation Flood [ऑपरेशन फ्लड] / White Revolution [श्वेत क्रांति] was launched in 1970 by the National Dairy Development Board (NDDB) under Dr. Verghese Kurien ('Father of the White Revolution in India'), establishing the Anand pattern of cooperative dairying (AMUL).
  • National Dairy Research Institute [राष्ट्रीय डेरी अनुसंधान संस्थान] (NDRI) is located at Karnal in Haryana, a premier deemed university in dairy sciences.
  • Stock farming is the practice of keeping पशुधन [Livestock] farm. पशुपालन [Animal Husbandry] is the scientific management of animal livestock. It includes various aspect such as feeding, breeding and disease control. Thus (b) is the correc... UPPSC

Cattle & Buffalo Breeds, Goat Rearing & UP Dairy Status

  • Uttar Pradesh ranks #1 among all Indian states in total milk production and possesses the largest buffalo population in the country.
  • Bhadawari [भदावरी] buffalo breed, native to the Chambal/Yamuna ravines of Agra and Etawah in UP and Bhind in MP, produces milk with the highest butterfat content (up to 12-13%) among all cattle breeds.
  • Murrah [मुर्राह] buffalo, originating in Haryana and Punjab, is known as 'Black Gold' for its exceptionally high daily milk yield.
  • Jamnapari [जमनापारी] goat breed, native to the ravines of Etawah district in Uttar Pradesh, is celebrated as India's premier dual-purpose (milk and meat) goat breed; goat is popularly termed the 'Poor Man's Cow' [गरीब की गाय].
  • Central Institute for Research on Goats [केंद्रीय बकरी अनुसंधान संस्थान] (CIRG) is situated at Makhdoom, Farah in Mathura district of Uttar Pradesh.
  • Indian Veterinary Research Institute [भारतीय पशु चिकित्सा अनुसंधान संस्थान] (IVRI) and Central Avian Research Institute (CARI) are both situated at Izatnagar, Bareilly in Uttar Pradesh.
