Chapter 24

Mineral Resources

India's mineral wealth is concentrated in Pre-Cambrian Dharwar rocks (metallic ores) and Gondwana sedimentary basins (coal). UPPSC questions focus on coal basins (Jharia, Raniganj, Singrauli), iron ore (Bailadila, Kudremukh), copper (Malanjkhand, Khetri), and UP minerals in Sonbhadra, Banda, and Prayagraj.

UPPSC asks most here

  1. Odisha is the largest (2024-25) coal producing state with a share of about 25.71%, followed by Jharkhand.GIC ’10; PCS Mains ’04; PCS Mains ’033 papers
  2. At the time when the question was asked, Gujarat was the leading producer of petroleum. As per the data of Annual Report 2024-25 (Ministry o...GIC ’10; PCS Mains ’04; PCS Mains ’033 papers
  3. Which of the following geological systems of rocks contain a rich reserve of iron ore in India? → Dharwar system.RO/ARO Mains ’14; PCS Mains ’122 papers

Geological Formations & Major Coalfields of India

  • Gondwana rock formations [गोंडवाना शैल] contain approximately 98% of India's total कोयला [Coal] reserves and over 99% of its annual coal production; primarily bituminous, non-coking coal.
  • Damodar Valley coalfield is the primary repository of metallurgical coking कोयला [Coal] in India; Jharia in Dhanbad district of Jharkhand is India's richest coalfield.
  • Raniganj in West Bengal is the oldest commercial कोयला [Coal] mine in India, where coal mining commenced in 1774 during the East India Company era.
  • Singrauli coalfield spans Sonbhadra district of Uttar Pradesh and Singrauli district of MP, supplying कोयला [Coal] to mega ताप विद्युत [Thermal Power] plants of NTPC.
  • Talcher in the Mahanadi valley (Odisha) and Singareni in the Godavari valley (Telangana) are major non-coking coalfields.
  • Neyveli in Cuddalore district of Tamil Nadu possesses the largest deposits of Lignite [लिग्नाइट] (brown कोयला [Coal]) in India, operated by NLC India Ltd.
  • Dharwar system is the most important mineralized rock system in India. These rocks are economically very important as they possess valuable minerals like high-grade iron-ore, manganese, copper, lead, gold, etc. UPPSC ×2
  • Gondwana system of Rocks provides 99.54% (April 1, 2022) of the कोयला [Coal] in India. The important coal-bearing areas of this series are Raniganj, Jharia, Karanpur and Bokaro of the Damodar Basin in Odisha, and the Pench Valle... UPPSC
  • लौह अयस्क [Iron Ore] in India is found mainly in Dharwar rock system . Dharwar- rock system is the most important rock system in terms of economic value in India. UPPSC
  • According to Indian Minerals Yearbook 2024, the only tin producing state in India is Chhattisgarh (2023-24, P). UPPSC

Metallic Ores: Iron Ore, Bauxite, Copper, Lead & Zinc

  • Dharwar rock system [धारवाड़ शैल तंत्र] is the oldest metamorphosed sedimentary rock formation in India and is the richest reservoir of metallic minerals (लौह अयस्क [Iron Ore], manganese, copper, lead, gold).
  • लौह अयस्क [Iron Ore] [लौह अयस्क] in India is predominantly high-grade Haematite (60-70% Fe) and Magnetite (black ore); Odisha is the leading producer (Barabil-Koira valley, Badampahar, Gorumahisani in Mayurbhanj).
  • Bailadila mines [बैलाडीला] in Dantewada district of Chhattisgarh produce super-high-grade haematite ore, exported to Japan through Visakhapatnam port; Kudremukh in Karnataka has major magnetite deposits.
  • बॉक्साइट [Bauxite] [बॉक्साइट] is the primary ore of aluminium; Odisha ranks #1 in India (Panchpatmali in Koraput district), followed by Gujarat and Jharkhand (Lohardaga).
  • Copper [तांबा] occurs in metamorphic rocks; Malanjkhand in Balaghat district of Madhya Pradesh is India's largest open-cast copper mine; Khetri in Jhunjhunu district of Rajasthan is a historic copper belt operated by HCL.
  • Rampur-Agucha in Bhilwara district and Zawar mines in Udaipur (Rajasthan) are India's richest Lead and Zinc deposits, operated by Hindustan Zinc Ltd.
  • Which of the following rock systems is the major source of metallic minerals in India? → Dharwar system. UPPSC
  • The ir on-ore reserves of India are associated with which of the following rock systems? → Dharwar. UPPSC
  • Which of the following geological systems of rocks contain a rich reserve of लौह अयस्क [Iron Ore] in India? → Dharwar system. UPPSC ×2
  • According to Indian Minerals Yearbook, 2024, among the states Odisha recorded the highest production of लौह अयस्क [Iron Ore] 148.96 million tonnes, i.e., about 53.71% of the country's production in 2023-24 (P). Chhattisgarh was at t... UPPSC

Mineral Wealth of Uttar Pradesh & Bundelkhand Deposits

  • Uttar Pradesh is generally poor in metallic minerals, but possesses strategic non-metallic and minor minerals concentrated in the southern Vindhyan and Bundelkhand plateau districts.
  • चूना पत्थर [Limestone] [चूना पत्थर] deposits are concentrated in Sonbhadra district (Guruma-Kanach-Basahari, Kajrahat, Rohtas belt), feeding major cement factories at Churk, Dalla, and Chunar.
  • बॉक्साइट [Bauxite] [बॉक्साइट] deposits in UP are found in Banda district (Rajhawan), Varanasi, and Lalitpur, utilized by the Hindalco aluminium plant at Renukoot.
  • Glass-sand / Silica Sand [कांच बालू] deposits are found extensively in Shankargarh [शंकरगढ़] (Prayagraj district - India's foremost glass-sand hub), Chitrakoot, and Banda.
  • Uranium and Copper traces are discovered in Lalitpur district of Uttar Pradesh.
  • Pyrophyllite and Diaspore are mined in Jhansi, Lalitpur, Hamirpur, and Mahoba districts; Dolomite is mined at Kajrahat and Bari in Sonbhadra district.

Miscellaneous : Minerals

India's non-metallic and hydrocarbon wealth includes mica, gypsum, offshore oil (Mumbai High), and inland refineries. UPPSC tests Koderma's historic mica dominance, Digboi (oldest oilfield), Mumbai High, Panna diamonds, and Mathura Refinery in UP.

Mica, Limestone, Gypsum, Atomic Minerals & Rare Earths

  • अभ्रक [Mica] [अभ्रक] (Muscovite / Ruby Mica) is an exceptional electrical insulator; Koderma in Jharkhand was historically celebrated as the 'Mica Capital of the World'; other deposits exist in Giridih (Jharkhand), Nellore (Andhra Pradesh), and Bhilwara (Rajasthan).
  • Majhgawan mine in Panna district [पन्ना] of Madhya Pradesh is the only active mechanized diamond mine in India, operated by NMDC.
  • Monazite sands [मोनाजाइट बालू] in coastal placer deposits of Kerala (Travancore) and Odisha are rich in Thorium, forming the backbone of Stage 3 of India's परमाणु ऊर्जा [Nuclear Power] programme.
  • Jaduguda [जादुगुड़ा] in East Singhbhum district of Jharkhand is India's first and premier commercial Uranium mine, operated by UCIL; Tummalapalle in YSR Kadapa (AP) has huge low-grade uranium reserves.
  • Gypsum [जिप्सम] is primarily produced in Rajasthan (Hanumangarh, Bikaner, Jodhpur), indispensable for cement manufacturing and Usar soil reclamation.
  • According to figure of Indian Minerals Yearbook 2024, Odisha has a monopoly of Chromite production. Rajasthan is the top silver producer (2023-24, P) in India. Odisha has the largest (April 1, 2020) reserves/resources of... UPPSC

Petroleum, Natural Gas Fields, Offshore Basins & Mathura Refinery

  • Crude oil was first discovered in India at Makum (Assam) in 1867; Digboi [डिगबोई] in Tinsukia district of Assam is the oldest operating oilfield and oil refinery in India, commissioned in 1901.
  • Mumbai High [बॉम्बे हाई] (discovered in 1974 by ONGC using the jack-up rig 'Sagar Samrat') is India's largest domestic offshore crude oil producing field in the Arabian Sea.
  • Ankleshwar in Bharuch district of Gujarat is a major onshore oilfield, once termed the 'Fountain of Prosperity' by Jawaharlal Nehru; Kalol, Mehsana, and Nawagam are other Gujarat fields.
  • Krishna-Godavari (KG-D6) Basin in the Bay of Bengal holds vast deepwater natural gas reserves; Barmer basin in Rajasthan (Mangala, Bhagyam, Aishwarya) produces substantial onshore crude.
  • Jamnagar Refinery (Reliance Industries in Gujarat) is the world's largest grassroots petroleum refining complex.
  • Mathura Oil Refinery [मथुरा तेल शोधनागार] (IOCL) in Uttar Pradesh was commissioned in 1982, processing crude transported via pipeline from Salaya (Gujarat); environmental emissions led to Supreme Court directives to protect the Taj Mahal from acid rain.
  • Metamorphic rocks are a result of a transformation of existing rock, in a process called metamorphism, which means "change in form". Metamorphic minerals are those that form only at the high temperatures and pressures as... UPPSC

  • India has not attained self-sufficiency in the production of any mineral mentioned in the question. According to data of Indian Minerals Yearbook 2024, world reserves of Graphite have been placed at 280 million tonnes of... UPPSC

← Chapter 24Electrical Energy →

Chapter 26

Electrical Energy

Thermal power provides ~55-60% of India's electricity, complemented by hydel, nuclear, and rapidly expanding solar energy. UPPSC focuses on NTPC super thermal stations (Singrauli, Rihand), Narora Nuclear Station (Bulandshahr, UP), and mega solar parks.

UPPSC asks most here

  1. Uranium is the most significant nuclear mineral used as nuclear fuel. Thorium is extracted mostly from monazite.PCS Mains ’05; Lower Sub ’02; PCS Pre ’033 papers
  2. The correct sequence of energy sources in order of their share in the power sector in India is as follows: As per the Ministry of Power, the...RO/ARO Mains ’14; UDA/LDA Pre ’102 papers
  3. The correct match of thermal projects and their locations is as follows – (Location) (State) Ukai - Gujarat Patratu - Jharkhand Pench - Madh...PCS Pre ’13; UDA/LDA Pre (Spl) ’102 papers

Thermal Power Stations & Hydroelectric Projects

  • ताप विद्युत [Thermal Power] accounts for approximately 55-60% of India's total installed power capacity, with कोयला [Coal] serving as the primary fuel.
  • National ताप विद्युत [Thermal Power] Corporation (NTPC, established in 1975) is India's largest power utility; its very first Super Thermal Power Station was built at Singrauli (Shaktinagar, Sonbhadra district, UP) in 1977.
  • Sonbhadra district [सोनभद्र] in Uttar Pradesh is popularly termed the 'Energy Capital of India' due to massive कोयला [Coal]-fired thermal complexes: Singrauli STPS, Rihand STPS, Obra ताप विद्युत [Thermal Power] Station, and Anpara Thermal Power Station.
  • जल विद्युत [Hydroelectric Power] is a clean, renewable energy source; Sidrapong near Darjeeling (1897) was the earliest hydel installation in India, followed by Sivanasamudra on Kaveri River in Karnataka (1902).
  • Tehri Dam (Uttarakhand - 2,400 MW complex), Bhakra-Nangal (HP/Punjab), Koyna (Maharashtra), and Sharavathi (Karnataka) are premier hydroelectric installations in India.
  • (Minerals) (Industries in which largely used) चूना पत्थर [Limestone] – Cement Copper – Electrical goods बॉक्साइट [Bauxite] – Aeroplane Manufacturing Manganese – Steel UPPSC
  • The ताप विद्युत [Thermal Power] unit of NTPC at Singrauli was established in the year 1977, while it has been since the year 1982. This is the first unit of NTPC. Singrauli region is spread over 2 districts of 2 states i.e. Sidhi in M... UPPSC
  • Idukki is not a ताप विद्युत [Thermal Power] project, but it is a जल विद्युत [Hydroelectric Power] project which is situated across the Periyar River in Idukki district, Kerala. Sabarigiri hydroelectric project is in Kerala. Ghatprabha is an irriga... UPPSC
  • Uran (Uran Gas Turbine Power Station) is a gas-based ताप विद्युत [Thermal Power] plant located in Raigad district of Maharashtra. UPPSC
  • Ramagundam Super ताप विद्युत [Thermal Power] Station, one of the biggest thermal power stations in India is situated in the then Andhra Pradesh (now Telangana). Its capacity is 2600 M.W. UPPSC

Nuclear Power Plants of India & Narora (Bulandshahr, UP)

  • परमाणु ऊर्जा [Nuclear Power] in India is managed by Nuclear Power Corporation of India Limited (NPCIL) under the Department of Atomic Energy (DAE).
  • Tarapur Atomic Power Station [तारापुर] in Maharashtra (commissioned in 1969 with American Boiling Water Reactors) was India's first commercial परमाणु ऊर्जा [Nuclear Power] station.
  • Narora Atomic Power Station [नरोरा परमाणु ऊर्जा संयंत्र] in Bulandshahr district, Uttar Pradesh, is the only परमाणु ऊर्जा [Nuclear Power] plant in UP, operating two indigenous 220 MW Pressurized Heavy Water Reactors (PHWR) using heavy water (D2O) as moderator and coolant from the Ganga.
  • Rawatbhata (Rajasthan - Canadian CANDU reactors), Kalpakkam / Madras Atomic Power Station (TN - India's first fully indigenous commercial nuclear plant), Kakrapar (Gujarat), and Kaiga (Karnataka) are operational nuclear stations.
  • Kudankulam परमाणु ऊर्जा [Nuclear Power] Plant in Tirunelveli district of Tamil Nadu operates two 1,000 MW VVER-1000 water-water energetic reactors built with Russian technical collaboration, possessing the highest unit capacity in India.

Renewable Energy: Solar Parks, Wind Power & Non-Conventional Sources

  • India targets 500 GW of non-fossil fuel installed electricity capacity by 2030 under its Panchamrit climate commitments made at COP26.
  • Bhadla Solar Park [भादला सोलर पार्क] in Jodhpur district of Rajasthan is one of the world's largest operational photovoltaic solar installations (~2,245 MW capacity).
  • Rewa Ultra Mega Solar project in Madhya Pradesh (750 MW) was one of the first to achieve grid parity; Pavagada Solar Park in Karnataka (2,050 MW) is another massive solar complex.
  • Muppandal Wind Farm in Kanyakumari district of Tamil Nadu is the largest onshore operational wind farm in India; Tamil Nadu and Gujarat lead in wind energy potential.
  • International Solar Alliance [अंतर्राष्ट्रीय सौर गठबंधन] (ISA), initiated jointly by India and France in 2015, is headquartered at Gurugram, Haryana.

Chapter 27

Industry

India's industrial landscape encompasses metallurgy, textiles, fertilizers, and locomotives. UPPSC highlights public sector steel plants (Bhilai, Rourkela, Durgapur, Bokaro), Hindalco Renukoot, BLW Varanasi, and UP's craft towns (Firozabad, Bhadohi, Moradabad, Aligarh).

Iron and Steel Industry: Plants, Locations & Foreign Collaborations

  • Iron and Steel industry [लौह एवं इस्पात उद्योग] is the basic heavy industry foundational to industrial infrastructure; TISCO (now Tata Steel) was established at Sakchi (Jamshedpur) by Jamsetji Tata in 1907.
  • During the Second Five-Year Plan (1956-61), three public sector integrated steel plants were erected with foreign assistance: Bhilai (Chhattisgarh) with Soviet (USSR) aid, Rourkela (Odisha) with West German aid (Krupp-Demag), and Durgapur (West Bengal) with British collaboration.
  • Bokaro Steel Plant (Jharkhand) was established during the Third Five-Year Plan in 1964 with Soviet (USSR) assistance.
  • Visakhapatnam Steel Plant (Rashtriya Ispat Nigam Ltd - RINL) in Andhra Pradesh is India's only shore-based integrated steel plant; Salem Steel Plant in Tamil Nadu produces specialized stainless steel.
  • Steel Authority of India Limited (SAIL) was incorporated in 1973 to manage public sector steel plants.
  • Petroleum, also called crude oil, is a fossil fuel. Like कोयला [Coal] and natural gas, petroleum was formed from the remains of ancient marine organisms, such as plants, algae, and bacteria. Industry i. Iron and Steel Industry *... UPPSC
  • Stainless steel is a metal alloy, made up of steel mixed with elements such as chromium, nickel, molybdenum, silicon, aluminium and carbon. Iron mixed with carbon to produce steel is the main component of stainless steel... UPPSC
  • Bhilai Steel Plant is the collaborative project of the Indian government and Russia, located in Bhilai, Durg district in Chhattisgarh. The plant was commissioned in 1959. Presently, the total requirement of लौह अयस्क [Iron Ore] for ... UPPSC

Textiles, Heavy Engineering & Specialized Industrial Clusters of UP

  • सूती वस्त्र उद्योग [Cotton Textile Industry] is India's oldest and largest agro-based industry; Mumbai was styled 'Cottonopolis of India'; Ahmedabad is the 'Manchester of India'; Kanpur was historically hailed as the 'Manchester of North India' [उत्तर भारत का मैनचेस्टर].
  • Hindalco Industries operates a major integrated aluminium smelting plant at Renukoot in Sonbhadra district (UP), powered by Rihand Dam hydel power and supplied by बॉक्साइट [Bauxite] from Jharkhand and MP.
  • Banaras Locomotive Works [बनारस रेल इंजन कारखाना] (BLW, formerly DLW) at Varanasi manufactures heavy diesel and electric railway locomotives; Modern Coach Factory is situated at Lalganj in Raebareli district.
  • Specialized industrial craft clusters of Uttar Pradesh tested repeatedly in UPPSC: Firozabad (glass bangles and glassware - सुहाग नगरी), Moradabad (brassware - पीतल नगरी), Bhadohi / Sant Ravidas Nagar (hand-knotted woollen carpets - कालीन नगरी), Aligarh (locks - ताला नगरी), and Kannauj (perfumes and attar - इत्र नगरी).
  • First modern chemical fertilizer plant in India was established in 1951 at Sindri in Dhanbad (Jharkhand); IFFCO operates large urea plants at Phulpur (Prayagraj) and Aonla (Bareilly) in Uttar Pradesh.

Chapter 28

Research Centres in India

Premier CSIR, ICAR, DRDO, and ISRO laboratories drive India's scientific and agricultural research. UPPSC questions heavily test institutional headquarters, with an extraordinary concentration of premier institutes located in Lucknow, Kanpur, Varanasi, and Jhansi.

National Scientific, Space, Oceanic & Agricultural Research Institutes

  • Indian Space Research Organisation (ISRO) is headquartered at Bengaluru; Satish Dhawan Space Centre (SDSC-SHAR) is located at Sriharikota in Tirupati district of Andhra Pradesh.
  • Bhabha Atomic Research Centre (BARC) is located at Trombay (Mumbai); Indira Gandhi Centre for Atomic Research (IGCAR) is at Kalpakkam (Tamil Nadu).
  • Forest Research Institute [वन अनुसंधान संस्थान] (FRI) and Indian Institute of Remote Sensing (IIRS) are situated at Dehradun in Uttarakhand.
  • Central Arid Zone Research Institute [केंद्रीय शुष्क क्षेत्र अनुसंधान संस्थान] (CAZRI) is located at Jodhpur in Rajasthan.
  • National Rice Research Institute [राष्ट्रीय चावल अनुसंधान संस्थान] (NRRI) is located at Cuttack in Odisha; Central Potato Research Institute (CPRI) is at Shimla; National Dairy Research Institute (NDRI) is at Karnal.
  • National Geophysical Research Institute (NGRI) is at Hyderabad; National Institute of Oceanography (NIO) is at Dona Paula, Goa; Central Mining and Fuel Research Institute (CIMFR) is at Dhanbad.
  • The cross border oil pipeline inaugurated by PM Modi in September 2019 will connect Motihari in India and Am- lekhganj in Nepal. UPPSC
  • The International Crops Research Institute for Semi-Arid Tropics (ICRISAT) is an international non-profit organization that undertakes scientific agrarian research for development in South Asia and Sub-Saharan Africa. Th... UPPSC
  • The Central Research Institute for Dryland Agriculture (CRIDA) is a national research institute under the Indian Council of Agricultural Research (ICAR) established in Hyderabad. UPPSC
  • National Academy of Agricultural Research Management (NAARM) is located at Hyderabad. UPPSC

Premier National Research Institutes Located in Uttar Pradesh

  • Lucknow hosts six premier national research institutes: Central Drug Research Institute [केंद्रीय औषधि अनुसंधान संस्थान] (CDRI), National Botanical Research Institute (NBRI), Central Institute of Medicinal and Aromatic Plants (CIMAP), Indian Institute of Sugarcane Research (IISR), Birbal Sahni Institute of Palaeosciences (BSIP), and Central Institute of Subtropical Horticulture (CISH).
  • Kanpur hosts two prominent institutions: Indian Institute of दलहन [Pulses] Research [भारतीय दलहन अनुसंधान संस्थान] (IIPR) and National Sugar Institute [राष्ट्रीय शर्करा संस्थान] (NSI).
  • Varanasi hosts the Indian Institute of Vegetable Research [भारतीय सब्जी अनुसंधान संस्थान] (IIVR) and Indian Institute of Handloom Technology (IIHT).
  • Jhansi hosts the Indian Grassland and Fodder Research Institute [भारतीय चरागाह एवं चारा अनुसंधान संस्थान] (IGFRI) and Central Agroforestry Research Institute (CAFRI).
  • Mathura hosts the Central Institute for Research on Goats [केंद्रीय बकरी अनुसंधान संस्थान] (CIRG) at Makhdoom.
  • Bareilly hosts the Indian Veterinary Research Institute [भारतीय पशु चिकित्सा अनुसंधान संस्थान] (IVRI) and Central Avian Research Institute (CARI) at Izatnagar.
  • Prayagraj hosts the Harish-Chandra Research Institute (HRI) for mathematics and theoretical physics, and Govind Ballabh Pant Social Science Institute.

Chapter 29

Transport in India

India maintains the world's 2nd largest road network, 4th largest railway system, 111 National Waterways, and 12 major seaports. UPPSC tests Golden Quadrilateral, the North-South & East-West corridor junction at Jhansi (UP), NW-1 (Prayagraj-Haldia), and Eastern DFC.

UPPSC asks most here

  1. The Bhagirathi - Hooghly portion of the Ganga river (or Ganges) system from 'Allahabad (Prayagraj) to Haldia' has been declared as National ...PCS Pre ’14; PCS Mains ’092 papers

Road Transport, Golden Quadrilateral & Jhansi Corridor Crossing

  • सड़क परिवहन [Road Transport] carries approximately 85% of passenger traffic and over 70% of freight traffic in India; National Highways Authority of India (NHAI) was constituted in 1988.
  • स्वर्णिम चतुर्भुज [Golden Quadrilateral] [स्वर्णिम चतुर्भुज] (5,846 km 4/6-lane highway network) connects the four mega-metropolises of India: Delhi, Mumbai, Chennai, and Kolkata.
  • North-South Corridor (Srinagar to Kanyakumari) and East-West Corridor (Silchar to Porbandar) intersect at Jhansi [झांसी] in Uttar Pradesh, a classic recurring UPPSC exam question.
  • NH-44 (formerly NH-7) is the longest National Highway in India (3,745 km), running from Srinagar (J&K) to Kanyakumari (Tamil Nadu); NH-19 (formerly NH-2 / historic Grand Trunk Road) connects Delhi to Kolkata traversing Mathura, Agra, Kanpur, Prayagraj, and Varanasi.
  • Uttar Pradesh is the 'Expressway State' of India, housing major operational access-controlled expressways: Yamuna Expressway, Agra-Lucknow Expressway, Purvanchal Expressway, Bundelkhand Expressway, and Ganga Expressway.
  • India has approximately 63.45 lakh kilometers of the road network, which is the second largest road network in the world. In the field of transport, roadways have a leading position. About 65% of freight and 80% of passe... UPPSC

Indian Railways, Dedicated Freight Corridors & UP Rail Hubs

  • Indian Railways operates across 18 Railway Zones; the first passenger train in India ran over a 34 km stretch between Bombay (Bori Bunder) and Thane on 16 April 1853 under Lord Dalhousie.
  • Shree Siddharoodha Swamiji Hubballi railway station (Karnataka, 1,507 m) holds the Guinness record for the longest railway platform in the world, having surpassed Gorakhpur railway station (UP, 1,366 m).
  • Two major railway zonal headquarters are located in Uttar Pradesh: North Eastern Railway [पूर्वोत्तर रेलवे] (NER) headquartered at Gorakhpur, and North Central Railway [उत्तर मध्य रेलवे] (NCR) headquartered at Prayagraj.
  • Eastern Dedicated Freight Corridor [ईस्टर्न डेडीकेटेड फ्रेट कॉरिडोर] (EDFC, running from Sahnewal/Ludhiana in Punjab to Dankuni in West Bengal) traverses Uttar Pradesh extensively through Saharanpur, Khurja, Tundla, Kanpur, and Pt. Deen Dayal Upadhyaya Junction (Mughalsarai).
  • Pt. Deen Dayal Upadhyaya Junction (formerly Mughalsarai) contains the largest railway marshalling yard in Asia and is the fourth busiest railway junction in India.
  • Pradhan Mantri Gram Sadak Yojana (PMGSY) was launched on 25th December 2000 as a fully funded Centrally Sponsored Scheme to provide all-weather road connectivity in rural areas of the country that are not connected. UPPSC
  • Among the given states, Maharashtra ranks first in India in terms of the length of roads. UPPSC
  • In the tenure of Lord Dalhousie, the country's first railway was built by the Great Indian Peninsula Railway (GIPR), opened on 16 April, 1853 between Bombay and Thane. UPPSC
  • After observing Indian railway map, it is clear that the shortest rail route of Mumbai from Gorakhpur runs through Allahabad (Prayagraj). UPPSC
  • The headquarters of Northern Railway is in New Delhi; hence, pair 1 is correct. The headquarters of North Eastern Railway is in Gorakhpur; hence, pair 2 is correct. The headquarters of South Eastern Railway is in Garden ... UPPSC

Inland Waterways (NW-1), Major Seaports & Civil Aviation

  • Inland Waterways Authority of India (IWAI) was established in 1986, headquartered at Noida in Uttar Pradesh.
  • राष्ट्रीय जलमार्ग-1 [National Waterway 1] (NW-1) is the longest and most significant national waterway in India (1,620 km), traversing the Ganga-Bhagirathi-Hooghly river system from Prayagraj (Uttar Pradesh) to Haldia (West Bengal).
  • India possesses 12 major government sea ports and 1 corporate major port (Ennore/Kamarajar): Western Coast ports: Deendayal/Kandla (Gujarat - tidal port), Mumbai Port, Jawaharlal Nehru Port / Nhava Sheva (Maharashtra - largest container port), Mormugao (Goa - लौह अयस्क [Iron Ore] export), New Mangalore (Karnataka), and Kochi (Kerala).
  • Eastern Coast ports: V.O. Chidambaranar / Tuticorin (TN), Chennai (oldest artificial harbour), Kamarajar/Ennore, Visakhapatnam (deepest natural landlocked port), Paradip (Odisha), and Syama Prasad Mookerjee / Kolkata-Haldia (riverine port).

Maritime Channels, Boundary Lines & Coastal Terminology

  • Strategic maritime channels tested by UPPSC: Ten Degree Channel [10° चैनल] (10° N latitude) separates Andaman Islands from Nicobar Islands; Nine Degree Channel [9° चैनल] separates Minicoy Island from the main Lakshadweep archipelago; Eight Degree Channel [8° चैनल] separates Minicoy (India) from Maldives.
  • Duncan Passage [डंकन पास] is a strait in the Indian Ocean separating South Andaman from Little Andaman.
  • Great Channel (Indira Point channel / 6° Channel) separates Great Nicobar Island from the Indonesian island of Sumatra (Rondo Island).
  • Palk Strait [पाक जलडमरूमध्य] and Adam's Bridge (Ram Setu) separate the Tamil Nadu coast of India from the Mannar district of Sri Lanka.
  • Sir Creek is a 96 km tidal estuary disputedly separating the Kutch region of Gujarat (India) from Sindh (Pakistan) in the Arabian Sea marshlands.

Geographical Curiosities, Ghost Towns & Significant Waterfalls

  • Famous 'Ghost Towns' [भूतहा शहर] of India: Kuldhara (abandoned 19th-century Paliwal Brahmin village in Jaisalmer, Rajasthan) and Dhanushkodi (Pamban Island, Tamil Nadu - destroyed by the 1964 Rameswaram चक्रवात [Cyclone]).
  • Charkhari [चरखारी] in Mahoba district of Uttar Pradesh is celebrated as the 'Kashmir of Bundelkhand' [बुंदेलखंड का कश्मीर] for its surrounding hills, historic forts, and scenic lakes, and is an active vibrant township (NOT a ghost town).
  • Kuttanad [कुट्टनाड] region in Alappuzha/Kottayam districts of Kerala features the lowest altitude in India (~2.2 meters below sea level); renowned for traditional Below Sea Level Farming, recognized by FAO as a Globally Important Agricultural Heritage System (GIAHS).
  • Chitrakote Falls [चित्रकूट जलप्रपात] on the Indravati River in Bastar district of Chhattisgarh is famously designated as the 'Niagara of India' [भारत का नियाग्रा] due to its broad horseshoe-shaped crest.
  • Kunchikal Falls on the Varahi River in Shimoga (Karnataka) and Nohkalikai Falls near Cherrapunji (Meghalaya - tallest plunge waterfall in India, 340 m) are notable water drops.

Universe Origin, Big Bang & Stellar Lifecycles

  • The first rational perspective regarding the origin of the planets was probably proposed by a French naturalist ‘Comte de Buffon’ in 1749. According to the number of stars participating in the origin of planets, the scientific concepts have been divided into two parts- (1) Monistic concept and (2) Dualistic concept.
  • The latest theory regarding the origin of the universe is the महाविस्फोट सिद्धांत [Big Bang Theory] Theory.
  • After the explosion, the universe expanded in all directions and different galaxies of different shapes were formed. Thus, the universe comprises of an infinite number of galaxies. It is estimated that there are appx. 100-200 billion galaxies in the universe and every galaxy is estimated to have around 100 billion stars. Our galaxy is known as an Akash Ganga or मंदाकिनी [Milky Way Galaxy].
  • In 2005, the Spitzer Space Telescope gave clear evidences that our galaxy (the मंदाकिनी [Milky Way Galaxy]) is barred spiral in shape, hence disapproving the earlier theories.
  • The formation of the star passes through different phases. In the first phase, a proto-star is formed after that the star enters the second phase i.e., the main sequence Star. A star of the size of our sun stays in this phase for around 9 or 10 billion years. After this phase, the fuel of the star i.e., hydrogen starts getting used up to an extent that the star enters the third phase i.e., towards its death.
  • In this phase, the outer layer of the star expands greatly, It becomes a Red Giant or Red Supergiant.
  • The "महाविस्फोट सिद्धांत [Big Bang Theory] Theory" explains the origin of the universe from the earliest known period through its subsequent large scale evolution. The measurement of the expansion rate of the universe places this moment approximatel... UPPSC
  • According to the theory propounded by Georges Lemaitre, 13.7 or 13.8 billion years ago the total cosmic substance was in the form of dense mass which spread to form a number of galaxies. An infinite group of these swirli... UPPSC
  • Subrahmanyan Chandrasekhar was an Indo-American physicist who described the “Chandrasekhar Limit”. This limit describes the maximum mass of a white dwarf star or the mass limit above which a star will ultimately collapse... UPPSC
  • According to the American space agency NASA, most black holes form the remnants of a large star that dies in a supernova explosion. UPPSC

The Solar System, Sun & Planetary Dynamics

  • Under the Monistic concept, Immanuel Kant’s Gaseous Hypothesis and Pierre-Simon Laplace’s Nebular Hypothesis became famous. Under the Dualistic concept, Chamberlin and Moulton Planetesimal Hypothesis, James Jeans and Jefferys Tidal Hypothesis, H.N. Russell and Lyttleton Binary Star Hypothesis etc. became noteworthy.
  • Georges Lemaitre (1894-1966) was the first to present the idea of an expanding universe. Later in 1967, Robert Wagoner presented the interpretation of this theory. The महाविस्फोट सिद्धांत [Big Bang Theory] theory explains how 13.7 or 13.8 billion years ago universe expanded from a very high density and high-temperature state.
  • The shape of the Galaxies varies in shape and size. Some are spiral, some are irregular and some are elliptical.
  • The universe is a collection of infinite galaxies. Every galaxy has around 100 billion stars. These stars are formed as a result of the presence of hydrogen and helium gases in their unified form (Nebulae). The gravitational force keeps the gases and dust particles in the unified form. Velocity, high temperature and organizational processes are the factors behind the formation of a star.
  • The lifespan of any star depends upon its size. A star of the size of our sun (one solar mass) has a lifespan of around 9 or 10 billion years. The bigger the star is, the shorter is its life span. For example, a star whose surface temperature is more than 30000 Kelvin and which is 100 times the size of our sun has a life span of only a few million years. After the main sequence life of the star ends, it enters in the elderly phase.
  • After the red Giant phase, a star witnesses an explosion in the form of a Nova or Supernova, thus entering its last stage of life. Depending upon its size it can end up in- a Black Dwarf, Neutron star or a कृष्ण विवर [Black Hole]. Sun like small stars – after the Red Giant phase and Nova explosion if the stellar mass is less than 1.44 solar Masses, the star will end up being a White Dwarf first and then into a Black Dwarf. Medium sized stars - after the Red Giant Phase and Supernova explosion, if the stellar mass is between 1.44 to 3 solar masses, then a star can convert into a Neutron Star (Pulsar). Large size stars – those which have a stellar mass more than 3 times that of our sun. These stars have the possibility to convert into a black hole after a supernova explosion.

Earth's Motions, Solstices, Equinoxes & Seasons

  • अक्षांश [Latitude] is the angular distance of a point on the earth’s surface, measured in degrees from the center of the earth.
  • The latitude angle is denoted by the Greek lowercase letter Phi (). 0o latitude is also known as the equator. It is halfway between the North Pole and the South Pole. The equator thus divides the planet into the Northern and Southern hemisphere. The earth is widest at the equator. All other latitudes are smaller than the equator.
  • The 23.5o N latitude in the Northern Hemisphere is known as the कर्क रेखा [Tropic of Cancer] whereas the 66.5o N latitude in the Northern Hemisphere is known as Arctic Circle.
  • The 0o latitude (equator) and 0o longitude (प्रधान मध्याह्न रेखा [Prime Meridian]) intersect each other in the Gulf of Guinea located on the western coast of Africa. The Gulf is located in the Atlantic Ocean. The north and south of any place on a globe or a map is determined by latitudes. The latitudinal value in degrees of any place is directly proportional to its extent in the north of the equator for example - Beijing (China) - 39o55'N, New Delhi - 28 o38'N, New York (USA) - 40 o45' N and Rome (Italy) - 41 o54' N. Hence among these Rome is the most northern city.

Rock Metamorphism, Permeability & the Rock Cycle

  • Igneous rocks are crystalline, unstratified, non-fossiliferous, and impervious to water, formed by magma cooling. UPPSC
  • Sedimentary rocks are characteristically deposited in distinct horizontal strata and contain biological fossils. UPPSC
  • Metamorphic transformation examples: Granite changes to Gneiss, Sandstone to Quartzite, and Limestone to Marble. UPPSC
  • Chalk is a highly permeable rock allowing water infiltration, whereas clay is largely impermeable. UPPSC

Volcanic Mechanics, Magma Types & Ring of Fire

  • (a) Both Statement-II and Statement-III are correct and both of them explain Statement-I (b) Both Statement-II and Statement-III are correct, but only one of them explains Statement-I (c) Only one of the Statements-II and-III is correct and that explains Statement-I (d) Neither Statement-II nor Statement-III is correct I.A.S. (Pr e) 2024 Ans. (a) Chemical weathering is the process that breaks down rock through chemical changes. Rainfall and temperature can affect the rate at which rocks weather. High temperatures and greater rainfall increase the rate of chemical weathering. Rocks in tropical regions exposed to abundant rainfall and hot temperatures weather much faster than similar rocks residing in cold, dry regions. Rainwater may contain impurities from the atmosphere in the form of dust particles and dissolved gases like oxygen, nitrogen and carbon dioxide. When water (e.g. rainwater) mixes with carbon dioxide gas in the air or in air pockets in soil, a weak acid solution, called carbonic acid, is produced. When carbonic acid flows through the cracks of some rocks, it chemically reacts with the rock causing some of it to dissolve. This process is called carbonation. It is very active in limestone regions. Rainwater contains dissolved oxygen from the atmosphere, which can participate in oxidation processes that contribute to weathering. Oxygen in rainwater reacts with minerals, particularly iron-bearing ones, to form oxides or rust, leading to weakening and breakdown. Hence, both Statement-II and Statement-III are correct and both of them explain Statement-I. The ज्वालामुखी [Volcano]
  • Among the gases emitted through a volcanic eruption the, highest quantity is that of water vapor (H2O). Other gases emitted are carbon-dioxide (CO2), sulphur dioxide (SO2), hydrogen (H2), carbon-monoxide (CO), hydrogen chloride (HCl), hydrogen fluoride (HF) and helium (He). Magma is made of molten rock and is stored in the Earth’s interior. lava is magma that reaches the Earth's surface with temperature ranging from about 600oC 1200oC. When Lava reaches the surface it cools to form igneous rocks. The composition of almost all lava of the earth’s crust is dominated by silica (SiO2) materials. Based on the silicate, lava can be classified into two types- Acidic Lava and Basic Lava.
  • Intrusive igneous rocks are formed when lava cools and solidifies before reaching the earth’s surface. Igneous rocks are of two types – intrusive and extrusive.
  • A geyser is a spring characterized by intermittent discharge of water ejected turbulently and accompanied by steam. Generally, all Geyser field sites are located near the active volcanic areas, and the geyser effect is due to the proximity of magma. For example the Old Faithful Geyser of Yellowstone National Park in the United States of America.
  • On the basis of size, the largest active volcano on Earth is Mauna Loa, situated on Hawaiian द्वीप [Islands]. Mount Etna is an active volcano on the east coast of the island of Sicily in Italy. It stands 3357 meters tall.

Earthquake Measurement (Richter/Mercalli) & Tsunamis

  • Primary (P) waves are longitudinal compressional waves that travel fastest and penetrate solid, liquid, and gas. UPPSC
  • Secondary (S) waves are transverse shear waves that cannot penetrate the molten outer liquid core. UPPSC
  • Surface (L) waves travel along the Earth's crust with the largest amplitude and cause catastrophic destruction. UPPSC

  • A continent is one of several very large land masses of the world. Block Mountains, Relict Ranges & Global Mountain Belts

  • A mountain range is a series of mountains arranged in a linear manner and connected by high ground.
  • Most geologists classify a mountain as a landform that rises at least 1000 metres or more above its surrounding areas, landforms having elevation less than 1000 metres are generally classified as hills. Thus those areas having an elevation of 1000 m above sea level and also a high relative height in that area are considered to be mountainous regions. Mountains occupy approx. 25% of the Earth's land area however supports only 12% of the global population.
  • Fold mountains – are mountains that are formed mainly by the effect of folding of layers within the upper part of the Earth’s crust.
  • The Rocky Mountain range is the most important mountain range of North America with an approximate length of 4800 km. The highest peak of the Rockies is Mount Elbert (4401 m). The Himalayas of South Asia are located at the northern most point of India. Their approximate length is 2500 km. The highest mountain peak in the world i.e., Mount Everest also known as Sagarmatha or Chomolungma (8848.86 m), along with the other highest peaks, is situated in the Himalaya Mountain Range.
  • The Alps are the highest and most extensive mountain range system that is entirely in Europe. Its highest peak is Mont Blanc (4807 mt). The Alps are the most prominent example of young fold Mountains.
  • Block mountains – Block mountains are formed as a result of faulting caused by tensile and compressive forces. They are also known as fault block mountains or horst mountains. Block mountains represent the upstanding part of the ground between two faults or on either side of a rift valley.
  • The Darling range is situated on the South Western coast of Australia. This range is located in Western Australia. UPPSC

Tropical & Subtropical Deserts (Sahara, Kalahari, Atacama, Thar)

  • मरुस्थल [Desert] is a barren area of landscape where little precipitation occurs and consequently, living conditions are hostile for plants and animals. About 1/3 rd of the land surface of the world is arid or semiarid. Regions receiving precipitation of less than 25 cm annually are categorized as deserts. Deserts can be classified by the amount of precipitation, by the temperature that prevails, by the causes of their dryness or by their geographical location.
  • According to German scientist Dr. Gerhard Knies, the amount of energy that is received from the sun by the word's desearts in 6 hours is more than the amount of energy consumed by the entire humankind in a year. Hence deserts are a very prominent source of renewable energy.

National & Continental Sobriquets

  • Geographical Sobriquets There are many countries in this world that are often known by their geographical sobriquets.
  • Around the summer solstice (approximately 21 June in the Northern Hemisphere and 22 December in the Southern Hemisphere), the sun is visible for a full 24 hours. Thus countries like Norway which lies in the Arctic region experience 24 hours of daylight, a reason why it is often called the Land of Midnight sun. Norway is the best-suited example of the phenomenon of Midnight Sun even though parts of other countries like Canada, Sweden, Russia, Iceland, and Finland are also located north of the Arctic Circle and experience a Midnight Sun.
  • Out of the 11131 species of birds found on this planet, around 3698 bird species are found in South America. Hence South America is also known as the 'Bird Continent'.
  • Thailand is the country that is also called the 'Land of white elephants', as it has a large number of White elephants and the white elephants are the symbol of royal power in Thailand. It is believed that if the King of Thailand was unsatisfied with the work of any of his associates, he would gift him a white elephant, which would turn the man bankrupt.
  • Thus Japan is known as the Land of the Rising Sun.
  • South Korea is known as the 'Land of The Morning Calm'. Istanbul, the former capital of Turkiye is a major tourist destination. Since it is a transcontinental city it is also known as the 'Gateway of the West'.

  • Finland is also called ‘the Garden of झीलें [Lakes]’or the Land of aThousand Lakes. More than one lakh lakes are found in Finland. UPPSC

High Mountain Average Height Sea Floar Area
  • Hypsographic curve describes the distribution of Earth's surface elevations and ocean depth relative to sea level. We read the hypsographic curve like a graph with an x-axis (horizontal) and a y-axis (vertical). The x-axis tells us the elevation of the province. The curved line on the graph tells us about the provinces - where we see a change in the slope of the line, we are looking at a different province.

  • The जलमंडल [Hydrosphere] is the total volume of water on the surface of our planet. Although the Earth’s Hydrosphere has been around for longer than 4 billion years, it continues to change and rise. This is caused by seafloor spreading and continental drift. Hydrosphere occupies more than 2/3 of the total surface of the Earth.

The salinity of the Ocean Almost every known chemical element can be found in varying proportions in the ocean.
  • Lands are the main source of salinity in the oceans and rivers are the main carriers. Other factors responsible for ocean salinity are - evaporation, wind, river water, precipitation, ocean currents and volcanoes etc.
  • The maximum contribution to the salinity of the ocean is that of sodium chloride.
  • The density of ocean water is directly proportional to its salinity. This means that if the density of water increases it is an indicator of an increase in salinity hence with the increase in water density, salinity also increases.
  • Maximum salinity is found between 20º-40º North अक्षांश [Latitudes] where salinity may increase up to 36‰.
  • Among the various seas and झीलें [Lakes] of the world, the Van झील [Lake] in Turkiye has a salinity of 330‰. The salinity of Dead Sea is 238‰.

The salinity of the Ocean Almost every known c... UPPSC

  • In 1884, during Challenger expedition, William Dittmar produced a report about the chemistry of sea water. He revealed in the report that there are 47 types of salts present in the sea water among which 7 are most promin... UPPSC

Hypersaline Water Bodies of the World (Lake Van, Dead Sea)

  • लवणता [Salinity] is the amount of salt dissolved in a body of water. On average, seawater in the world’s oceans has a salinity of approximately 35‰ or 35 parts per thousand. This means that for every 1 kg (1000 grams) of seawater there are 35g of salt dissolved in it.
  • During an exploration of Challenger Deep in 1884, Dittmar discooured that there are 47 types of salts present in the seas. Out of these 47 types of salts, 7 are the important ones. They are as follows. Salt लवणता [Salinity] (‰) Percentage (%) of total dissolved salt Sodium Chloride 27.213 77.8 Magnesium Chloride 3.807 10.9 Magnesium Sulphate 1.658 4.7 Calcium Sulphate 1.260 3.6 Potassium Sulphate 0.863 2.5 Calcium Carbonate 0.123 0.3 Magnesium Bromide 0.076 0.2
  • The salinity of the ocean can be divided into horizontal and vertical parameters. The uncertainty regarding the amount of salinity in the vertical dimension has obstructed the devlopment of a principle for measuring vertical salinity. This is because in some places, salinity increases with depth and vice-versa.
  • The horizontal distribution of salinity in ocean water is expressed as the degree of saltiness of the water, either as a percentage or more often in parts per thousand Variations are shown in salinity distribution maps by isohalines (lines joining places having an equal degree of salinity). अक्षांश [Latitudes] have a huge impact on the salinity of the oceans. लवणता [Salinity] is lower than the average 34‰ in equatorial waters because of the heavy daily rainfall and high evaporation. मरुस्थल [Deserts] mainly 25º–30º N and S, waters have high salinity because of the high rate of evaporation caused by high temperatures and low humidity. Waters of the polar region have a salinity of less than 35‰ due to the colder climate with little evaporation and because much fresh water is added from the melting of icebergs as well as by several large poleward-bound rivers, e.g. Ob, Lena, Yenisei etc.
  • Ocean water of the Indian Ocean falling between 0º-10º अक्षांश [Latitudes] has a salinity of 35.14‰ however it decreases to 30‰ in Bay of Bengal. On the contrary the salinity increases upto 36‰ in the Arabian Sea. This is due to a high rate of evaporation and low humidity. Additionally there is much less fresh water carried to the Arabian Sea by west flowing rivers as compared to Bay of Bengal.
  • लवणता [Salinity] in the Northern part of Caspian Sea is 13-14‰, whereas in the southern part of Caspian sea, in the Karabuga Bay, salinity is 300-350‰.
  • Among the lakes and seas given in the question, झील [Lake] Van of Turkiye has maximum salinity which is 330‰. लवणता [Salinity] in the Dead Sea = 238‰ Salinity in the Red Sea = 36-41‰ Salinity in Great Salt Lake = 220‰ UPPSC

  • When the Sun, the Moon and the Earth are aligned in a straight line, we experience a दीर्घ ज्वार [Spring Tide]. In astronomy, this situation is known as syzygy. It occurs on a full moon or new moon day. On the other hand, when the Sun, the Moon and the Earth are at a right angle to each other the gravitational forces of these celestial bodies act against each other resulting in लघु ज्वार [Neap Tide]. Neap Tide is observed on the 7th or 8th day of each lunar month (14 days appx.) During Neap ज्वार-भाटा [Tides], there is a decrease of 20% in the Spring Tides whereas the Neap Tides are higher than the normal occurrences.
  • The effect of Moon on the tidal forces of our planet is 2.17 times greater than that of the Sun.

Spring Tides, Neap Tides & High-Range Estuaries (Fundy)

  • ) Red Sea has the highest salinity (36 to 41‰). In the above options, the Mediterranean Sea is also a sea with a salinity of 37 to 39‰. When the density in the sea incr eases, then – (a) लवणता [Salinity] and depth decreases. (b) Salinity increases but depth decreases. (c) Both salinity and depth increases. (d) Salinity decreases and depth increases. U.P. P.C.S. (Pre) 1990 Ans. (
  • ज्वार-भाटा [Tides] are the rise and fall of sea levels caused by the combined effects of the gravitational forces exerted by the Moon and the Sun, and the rotation of the Earth. The rotation of the Earth creates two forces - the centripetal and centrifugal forces, which play a major role in the formation of tides. This means that the side of the Earth that faces the Moon experiences a gravitational force, greater than that of the Earth itself. Hence we experience high tides. On the other hand the side of Earth that is facing away from moon experiences less gravitational force from the Moon, as a result, we experience high (indirect high tides) tides. Between these two high tides water level goes down this is called low tides (ebb). Thus in the time span of 24 hrs. and 50 minutes (or 52 minutes) we experience two High Tides and two Low Tides. Moon Syzygy Sun Moon Moon Sun Moon
  • The Moon is the closest celestial body to Earth hence its gravity has a significant impact on our planet. The moon is responsible for the sea waves of the oceans.
  • The city of Southampton in England experiences 4 tides every day. This occurs because two tides travel from English Channel and two from the North Sea at different intervals.
  • The gravitational tide-generating force of the Moon is approximately 2.17 times greater than that of the Sun due to its proximity. UPPSC
  • Spring tides occur during syzygy (New Moon and Full Moon) when Sun, Moon, and Earth align in a straight line, producing maximum tidal range. UPPSC
  • Neap tides occur during quadrature (First and Third Quarter phases) when gravitational forces act at right angles, resulting in lowest tidal range. UPPSC
  • The Bay of Fundy in Nova Scotia, Canada experiences the highest tidal range in the world, with tides reaching 16 to 18 meters. UPPSC

Whi ch of the following pairs is not correctly matched? (a) Canyon - River (b) Zeugen - W ind (c) Inselber g - Glacier (d) Moraine - Glacier M.P . P.C.S. (Pre) 2017 Ans. (c) Canyons are formed by rivers. They take the form of a V-shaped valley, in which the side wall have a steep slope and the depth is greater than the width. Moraines are formed by the depositional action of glaciers. Glaciers transport Material ranging from fine particles to large boulders. When the glacier cannot carry away these substances, they get deposited and this deposit is called a moraine. In addition, zeugen and inselberg are produced by wind. In deserts, soft rock is easily cut away by weathering and erosion, but hard rock remains as remnants in the form of dunes, which are called inselbergs. As a result of the erosion process of the wind, in the desert shapes resembling covered feasts are formed from the rocks. Such land, forms are called 'Jurgen'. Hence, it is clear that option (c) is not correctly matched. 7. Nobi and Kanto plains are located in which of the following countries? (a) North Korea (b) South Korea (c) Japan (d) China U.P .P.C.S. Coral Reef Biology, Environmental Conditions & Symbiosis

  • Coral reefs are called the rainforest of the ocean.
  • Coral reefs, constitute only about 0.2% of the seafloor, however, they provide for 25% of the oceanic flora and fauna.
  • When the temperature rises the corals start removing the zooxanthellae from their bodies. As a result, the corals turn white and this phenomenon is called coral bleaching.
  • Fringing reefs are comparatively narrower. This reef system lies close to the shore hence, wherever the river meets the ocean the fringing reef breaks. Fringing reefs are found in South Florida, Malaysia, etc.
  • Hoover dam also known as Boulder dam is a concrete arch- gravity dam in the Black Canyon of Colorado River on the border of U.S. states of Nevada and Arizona. Thus, option (b) is not correctly matched. The प्रवाल भित्ति [Coral Reefs] *C... UPPSC

The Great Barrier Reef, Atoll Formation & Coral Bleaching

  • Coral reefs are found in abundance in the tropical latitudes i.e. between कर्क रेखा [Tropic of Cancer] and the मकर रेखा [Tropic of Capricorn] (generally extending between 30 0N to 30 0S). They occur in shallow tropical areas where the seawater is clean, clear and warm.
  • Coral is actually a living organism.
  • ग्रेट बैरियर रीफ [Great Barrier Reef] is the largest coral reef in the world. It is located in the Pacific Ocean, North-East of the Australian state of Queensland. The Great Barrier Reef supports extraordinary biodiversity, including more than 450 species of hard coral, 1625 species of fish and over 3000 species of molluscs.
  • An atoll is a ring shaped coral reef that sometimes looks like a horseshoe. Atolls are found in Antilles sea, Red sea, Indonesia Sea, China Sea and Australian Sea. Funafuti वलयाकार प्रवाल भित्ति / एटोल [Atoll] is a famous atoll.
  • The ग्रेट बैरियर रीफ [Great Barrier Reef] off Queensland, Australia is the world's largest coral reef ecosystem, extending over 2,300 kilometers. UPPSC
  • Reef-building corals require warm, shallow, transparent tropical waters with temperatures between 20°C and 25°C and salinity of 27–35‰. UPPSC
  • Coral bleaching occurs when corals expel symbiotic Zooxanthellae algae under elevated sea surface temperatures or environmental stress. UPPSC
  • The ग्रेट बैरियर रीफ [Great Barrier Reef] is a site of remarkable variety and beauty on the north-east coast of Australia. Notably, it con- tains the world's largest collection of coral reefs, with 450 types of coral, 1625 species of fish ... UPPSC

  3. In the vertical structure of the atmosphere, Troposphere is the lowermost layer. Its average elevation is about 12 km. All seas...RO/ARO Pre ’161 paper

Atmospheric Composition, Trace Gases & Greenhouse Effect

  • The atmosphere is composed of various gases, of which 99% is made up of nitrogen and oxygen. Other 1% comprises argon, carbon dioxide, neon, krypton, helium, Hydrogen, and xenon. Gas Formula Presence in वायुमंडल [Atmosphere] (in percentage) Nitrogen N 2 78.084 Oxygen O 2 20.947 Argon Ar 0.934 Carbon dioxide CO2 0.0426 Neon Ne 0.0018 Helium He 0.0005 Krypton Kr 0.0001 Hydrogen H 2 0.000053 Xenon Xe 0.000009 ∗Heavy gases in the atmosphere descend whereas lighter gases are found in the upper layers as they ascend. As a result CO2 gases extend up to 20 km from the surface, oxygen, and nitrogen up to 100 km, hydrogen up to 125 km and other gases rise far above 125 km except ozone. The atmosphere is made up of different layers of gases with varying densities and temperatures. Based on these factors different layers of the atmosphere are as follows- (1) क्षोभमंडल [Troposphere] – The lowest layer of the atmosphere is called troposphere. All weather activities like clouds, hail, fog, blizzards, thunder, windstorms, etc. are confined to the troposphere. The most important characteristic of the troposphere is that there is a decrease of 6.5 o C with an increase of every 1000 m in altitude. This decrease in temperature with an increase in altitude is known as normal lapse rate. The upper boundary of the troposphere is called tropopause. (2) समतापमंडल [Stratosphere] – Stratosphere (15-50 km) is the second most important layer of the atmosphere. It lies between mesosphere (above) and troposphere (below). This layer of atmosphere is very dry in nature and has the least amount of water vapor in it. As a result, there is no weather activity in stratosphere. The stratosphere is used for aviation purposes as the absence of weather activities makes this layer ideal for flying. Ozone layer is found in this sphere. (3) मध्यमंडल [Mesosphere] – This layer is found at an altitude of 50 to 80 km. The upper ceiling of mesosphere is called mesopause. (4) आयनमंडल [Ionosphere] – It is an electrically conducting layer, that makes radio wave transmission possible over long distances. Ionosphere extends from 80 to 640 km.

  • Permanent gases in the atmosphere are those gases whose quantity does not change from day to day. These gases pri- marily contain nitrogen, oxygen and argon. Except for these three gases, the quantity of CO 2 ozone and n... UPPSC

(5) बहिर्मंडल [Exosphere] – It is the topmost layer of the atmosphere and is situated above the ionosphere. This layer has no defined boundary. The Communication satellites are positioned in the exosphere.

The outer layer of the Sun i.e. the photosphere releases energy at a constant rate. As a result, each per unit area of the Earth also receives constant solar energy. This phenomenon is known as the solar constant. ∗ Distribution of Solar Energy – Normally the solar energy received decreases from the equator towards poles. However, there are temporal changes in the energy received at every latitude. Since the Sun’s position in comparison to that of the Earth shifts northwards and southwards during Earth’s revolution due to Earth's tilta the maximum solar energy received also shifts north and south of the equator in the same respect. 20o North अक्षांश [Latitude] receives the maximum solar energy not the equator. ∗Regions where the Sun’s rays fall in a slanted manner receive less solar energy compared to regions where the Sun’s rays are falling vertically. ∗धवलता [Albedo] is the measure of how much light that hits a surface is reflected without being absorbed. Earth has an average albedo of 30% whereas the maximum albedo effect is produced by surfaces such as glaciers and snow (75-95%). मरुस्थल [Deserts] have an albedo of 20-30%, paddy fields 3-15% and prairies 10-20%. ∗Water vapors are capable of absorbing the infrared radiation waves and the outgoing long waves of the dispersed solar waves however, water vapors are transparent to the ultraviolet rays of the sun, hence are incapable of absorbing them.

Dew Point Condensation, Saturation & Sultry Weather

  • Relative humidity is the ratio of actual water vapour present in the air to the maximum moisture the air can hold at that temperature. UPPSC

  • When relative humidity is very high in summer alongside high temperature, the human body feels muggy, sultry, and oppressive heat. UPPSC
  • The dew point is the critical temperature at which a given parcel of air reaches 100% saturation and condensation begins. UPPSC

Atmospheric Pressure Principles, Measurement & Isobars

  • Atmospheric pressure is measured using a barometer. It is measured, as the force exerted per unit area of the barometer. A sudden fall in the level of barometer is a sign of an upcoming storm. If the readings of a barometer fall and then start rising slowly it denotes that there will be rainfall.
  • The horizontal distribution of atmospheric pressure is shown by using isobars.
  • आर्द्रता [Humidity] is the amount of water vapour in the air. In summer, humidity is very high therefore weather is muggy. Absolute humidity is the weight of water vapour per unit volume, usually expressed in grains per cubic foot. UPPSC

Global Pressure Belts: Doldrums & Horse Latitudes

  • A continuous rise in the level of mercury in the barometer is a sign of an anticyclone and clear weather.
  • An isobar is an imaginary line that connects points having same atmospheric pressure at mean sea level. ∗Based on the process of origin, pressure belts can be divided into two parts- (1) Based on thermogenesis – pressure belts thus formed are equatorial low-pressure belts and polar high-pressure belts. (2) Based on motion-pressure belts thus formed are sub- tropical high-pressure belts and sub-polar low-pressure belts. ∗Equatorial Low-Pressure Belts – The belt extends between 5o north and south of Equator. The equator receives vertical rays of the sun throughout the year. As a result, the air gets heated and starts rising up. Hence the region always experiences low pressure. The surface air of the region moves with very slow velocity as a result of which the environment here is quite calm. This is the reason this pressure belt is also called शांत कटिबंध / डोलड्रम्स [Doldrums]. • Sub-tropical High-Pressure Belt – the belt is found between 30o-35o latitudes in both hemispheres. The air rising from the equator starts becoming cooler in the upper layers of atmosphere and hence begins sinking near 30o-35o latitudes. Simultaneously the air from the sub-polar low-pressure belts also starts descending in this region due to Earth's rotation thus a high-pressure zone is created which gives birth to sub- tropical high pressure belt. The pressure belt is highly mobile in nature, thus it is also called Horse अक्षांश [Latitude]. • Sub-Polar Low-Pressure Belt – This pressure belt is found between 60o-65o latitudes in both hemispheres. Though the temperature in this region is low throughout the year the region experiences low pressure. This is due to the fact that rotation of the Earth causes the air to spread and displaces it. As a result low pressure is created. • Polar High-Pressure Belts – The poles and the regions in close proximity to them experience high pressure because the region is very cold which causes the air to become heavy and start descending creating high pressure.
  • Atmospheric pressure at mean sea level averages 1013.25 millibars and is measured using a mercury or aneroid barometer. UPPSC
  • The शांत कटिबंध / डोलड्रम्स [Doldrums] (Equatorial Low-Pressure Belt) is characterized by intense solar heating, rising convectional air, and light variable calms. UPPSC
  • The Horse अक्षांश [Latitudes] (Subtropical High-Pressure Belts at 30°–35° N and S) feature descending dry air where major world deserts are located. UPPSC

Wh ich of the following latitudes (north or south) would you associate with 'Horse अक्षांश [Latitudes]' ? (a) 30° (b) 45° (c) 60° (d) 23 21 0 U.P. Lower (Spl) (Pre) 2008 Ans. (a) The horse latitudes are located at about 30° to 35° north and south of the Equator. In the northern hemisphere it is called the north subtropical high-pressure belt and in the southern hemisphere, it is known as the south subtropical high-pressure belt. The existence of these pressure belts is due to the fact that the rising air of the equatorial region is deflected towards poles due to the earth’s rotation. After becoming cold and heavy, it descends in these regions due to Earth rotation and gets piled up. This results in high pressure. Calm conditions with feeble and variable winds are found here. In ancient times vessels with a cargo of horses passing through these belts found difficulty in sailing under these calm conditions. They used to throw a few horses in to the sea in order to make the vessels lighter. Thus, these belts or latitudes came to be known as ‘horse latitudes’. 3. Consider the following statements:

Forces Governing Winds: Coriolis Effect & Ferrel's Law

  • The movement of air as a result of the difference in horizontal air pressure is known as wind. Wind in general flows from a high- pressure area to a low-pressure area. If the earth were still and flat, the winds would blow from a high-pressure area directly to a low-pressure area along the isobaric lines at an angle of 90o. However, the Earth is geoid in shape and rotates on its own axis. Hence winds under the influence of dynamic forces keep shifting their trajectory. Types of Winds – There are basically three types of wind- Planetary Winds – Winds blowing constantly in a particular direction throughout the year are called planetary winds. The main planetary winds are – trade winds, westerlies and polar easterlies.
  • पछुआ पवनें [Westerlies]- They blow from sub-tropical high-pressure belts (30o-35o) toward sub-polar low-pressure belts (60 o-65o) in both the hemisphere. Since there is more landmass in Northern Hemisphere compared to Southern Hemisphere the westerlies in the former case are more complex, and they are less influential in summers as compared to winter.
  • Wellington (New Zealand) is the only capital in the world that lies in the path of Roaring Forties.
  • Land and Sea Breezes only cover 20 to 30 km of the area including the coastal area. During daytime the land heats up faster than the nearby sea as a result low pressure is created over land and thus cool and moist winds blow from sea to land. This wind is called a sea breeze . There is a complete reversal in the direction of the wind during the night. As a result, winds blow from land to sea. This wind is called Land breeze. • Mountain and घाटी [Valley] Breeze - During daytime the air heats up and rises along the slope of the mountain. This is known as Valley Breeze (Anabatic flow). During the night the air gets cooler and heavier as a result it starts descending along the slope of the mountain. This is known as Mountain Breeze (Katabatic flow). (3) स्थानीय पवनें [Local Winds: चिनूक पवन [Chinook: Snow Eater], फ़ॉन पवन [Foehn: Warm dry alpine wind], सिरोको पवन [Sirocco: Blood rain wind], मिस्ट्रल पवन [Mistral: Cold Rhone wind], Khamsin] – These winds are generated as a result of regional differences in temperature or air pressure. Hence, they affect a very small area. ∗The Mediterranean Sea is located between 30o to 40o North अक्षांश [Latitudes]. Hence, the sea comes under the influence of the sub-tropical high-pressure belt. This belt is mobile in nature. During summers, the trade winds blow in these latitudes and in winter these latitudes come under the influence of westerlies. This means that the westerlies blow towards North east from this region and व्यापारिक पवनें [Trade Winds] blow towards south west from this region.
  • Coriolis force is the force that is responsible for the deflection of the winds in the Northern and Southern Hemispheres. It is also known as the impact of Ferrel’s law. According to Ferrel’s law, any moving fluid turns ... UPPSC
  • The Mediterranean Sea is located in the Northern Hemisphere between 300 - 400 latitude. This area is under a subtropical high-pressure dynamic belt. Summer trade winds and westerly(anti-trade) winter winds blow from thes... UPPSC

Global Planetary Winds: Trade Winds, Westerlies & Roaring Forties

  • Trade winds are winds blowing from sub- tropical high-pressure belts toward equatorial low-pressure belts in both the hemispheres.
  • Due to the presence of less landmass in the Southern Hemisphere the westerlies travel at such a high speed causes them to become stormy. It is because of this high intensity of the westerlies in the southern hemisphere that around 40o latitudes they are known as Roaring Forties, around 50o latitude they are called Furious Fifties and around 60o latitude they are called Shrieking Sixties.
  • Polar winds blow from polar high-pressure belt to sub-polar low-pressure belt. Periodic winds – Winds are those that reverse their direction as per season or time. The important periodic winds are – monsoonal winds, land and sea breezes, breezes of mountains and valleys. Monsoonal winds are those that completely reverse their direction according to seasons. During the six months of summer they blow from sea to land and during the six month of winter they blow from land to sea.
  • The Coriolis force causes winds to deflect to the right in the Northern Hemisphere and to the left in the Southern Hemisphere (Ferrel's Law). UPPSC
  • Trade winds blow steadily from the Subtropical High-Pressure belts toward the Equatorial Low as North-East and South-East winds. UPPSC
  • पछुआ पवनें [Westerlies] in the Southern Ocean face minimal land resistance, generating the fierce 'Roaring Forties', 'Furious Fifties', and 'Shrieking Sixties'. UPPSC

Hot & Dry Local Winds: Chinook, Foehn, Sirocco & Harmattan

  • Local winds are generated as a result of pressure and temperature gradient in a particular region and hence affect a very small area. These winds are confined to the lower level of the troposphere. Important local winds are as Follows :
  • Santa Ana is a local wind prevailing in Southern California. It is also a warm and dry wind like चिनूक पवन [Chinook: Snow Eater] but unlike Chinook, it blows as a Channel Wind in the valley east to west.
  • सिरोको पवन [Sirocco: Blood rain wind] is a local wind that blows from the Sahara मरुस्थल [Desert] towards the Mediterranean Sea. The wind is dry, warm and sandy in nature. This air contains a lot of red sand. When blowing over the Mediterranean Sea the wind absorbs moisture and occasionally provides the southern Italy region with rainfall. When red particles descends along with rain it is known as Blood Rain. The sand carried by Sirocco from Libya is transported to Malta, Italy, Spain, Croatia, Montenegro, Albania and Greece.
  • मिस्ट्रल पवन [Mistral: Cold Rhone wind] is a cold polar wind that blows around the northwestern part of the Roum Sea, affecting the areas of Spain and France.
  • Loo is a dry and hot local wind that prevails in northern India. It blows from northwest and west to the east. These winds are tropical and dry and travel at a high velocity.
  • Zonda is a warm dry wind that blows along the eastern slopes of the Andes Mountains and influences the country of Argentina.
  • फ़ॉन पवन [Foehn: Warm dry alpine wind]- A warm dry wind blowing down the northern slopes of the Alps. It most influences the weather of Switzerland. Simoom- A strong, dry wind that blows in the deserts of Arabia and Kurdistan. Santa Ana - The local wind... UPPSC
  • चिनूक पवन [Chinook: Snow Eater] - It is a local wind that blows in the east of the Rockies mountains, USA and Canada. सिरोको पवन [Sirocco: Blood rain wind] - A wind that blows from the Sahara मरुस्थल [Desert] toward the northeast direction of the Mediterranean Sea and enters Sicily (... UPPSC
  • सिरोको पवन [Sirocco: Blood rain wind] is a warm, dry and dusty wind blowing from Sahara मरुस्थल [Desert] to the Mediterranean Sea. This wind carries red sand with it. This wind sometimes causes rain in Italy. When the red sand falls to the earth with the raind... UPPSC
  • सिरोको पवन [Sirocco: Blood rain wind] is a Mediterranean wind that blows from the Sahara मरुस्थल [Desert]. Sirocco carries dust from Libya to Malta, Italy, Croatia, Montenegro, Albania and Greece. Therefore, it is not related to France. UPPSC

Cold Local Winds: Mistral, Bora, Blizzard, Pampero & Buran

  • चिनूक पवन [Chinook: Snow Eater] means snow-eater. These winds descend along the eastern slope of the Rocky Mountains and are warm and dry in nature. These winds increase the temperature of the region during winters resulting in the melting of snow and the emergence of fresh greenery in the region. The winds rising along the southern slope of Alps descend along the northern slope and are warm and dry in nature. It is called फ़ॉन पवन [Foehn: Warm dry alpine wind] in Europe and has its maximum influence in Switzerland.
  • हरमट्टन पवन [Harmattan: Doctor wind] is a local wind that blows in a northeast and east direction in the western parts of Sahara मरुस्थल [Desert]. It is also dry and dusty air. With the advent of Harmattan, the weather becomes dry, pleasant and healthy. It is the reason why Harmattan is also called Doctor Wind in the coastal areas of Guinea.
  • Simoom is a local wind that blows in the deserts of Arabia and Kurdistan.
  • बोरा पवन [Bora: Cold Adriatic wind] is a dry cold air that moves at a very fast pace. It prevails along the eastern coasts of Adriatic Sea. The northern part of Italy is highly influenced by Bora.
  • Norwesters are pre monsoon thunderstorms blowing in India which are also known as Kalbaisakhi.
  • Black Roller is a local wind prevailing in the Great Plains region of North America.
  • बोरा पवन [Bora: Cold Adriatic wind] is a dry, cold and fast blowing wind on the eastern coast of the Adriatic Sea. The northern part of Italy is particularly influenced by this wind. Thus, option (b) is not correctly matched. Other options are correct... UPPSC
  • Shamal is a hot, dry and dusty wind blowing mainly from June to August, which blows to the north or northwest in Iraq, Iran and the Arabian Peninsula. Its speed is approx. 45 miles per hour. UPPSC

  • Forests are not only natural resources but are an essential part of our environment. They play a vital role in balancing the ecosystem.
  • According to the data of Global Forest Resources Assessment, 2025 total forest area of Bhutan is estimated to be 70.13% (2.67 million ha.) of the total land area while 29.87% (1.14 million ha.) of the total land area is estimated to be non-forest area in 2025.
  • The rain forests of Amazon Basin are the single largest remaining tropical rainforest in the world. This forest occupies at least 60% of the area of South America. If the tropical evergreen forest is cut down it does not regrow at the same pace as that of tropical deciduous forest. The reason is that excessive rain makes the soil of the tropical evergreen forests poor in nutrients.
  • FAO has defined softwood as coniferous. Conifer is a softwood tree found in abundance in the Baltic Region (Scandinavia and Russia), North America and China.

Temperate, Mediterranean & Taiga / Boreal Coniferous Biomes

  • Bhutan is one of the smallest countries in the world, but its commitment to conservation is bigger than most. Conservation of the environment is one of the four pillars of Bhutan's Gross National Happiness Philosophy. As mandated in its constitution, Bhutan preserves (at all times) 60 percent of its land under forest cover.
  • Top 10 countries in the world with the largest forest area in the year 2025 are as follows- Forest area for top ten countries in 2025 S. No. Country Forest area (000 ha) % world forest area 1 Russian Federation 832630 20 2 Brazil 486087 12 3 Canada 368819 9 4 USA 308895 7 5 China 227153 5 6 Democratic Republic of the Congo 139189 3 7. Australia 133562 3 8 Indonesia 95969 2 9 India 72739 2 10 Peru 67160 2 Top 10 Countries for annual net gain in forest area, 2015-2025 Forest Area Ranking Country 1000 ha/yr Percentage 1 China 1686 0.77 2 Russian Federation 942 0.11 3 India 191 0.27 4 Turkiye 118 0.53 5 Australia 105 0.08 6 France 95.9 0.56 7 Indonesia 94.1 0.10 8 South Africa 87.6 0.39 9 Canada 82.5 0.02 10 Vietnam 72.8 0.51 • Tropical Evergreen Forests – They are found on both the sides of the equator between 10 oN and 10oS latitudes. The equatorial region remains hot throughout the year and as a result, there is a lot of rainfall throughout the year. Hence, the equatorial region is covered with evergreen forests. These forests are found in abundance in three diverse regions of the world – (1) Amazon Basin – The region includes Brazil, Bolivia, Peru, Ecuador, Colombia, Venezuela, Guyana and Suriname. (2) Congo Basin – The region includes the Republic of Congo, Cameroon, Central African Republic, Gabon, Equatorial Guinea and Democratic Republic of Congo. (3) Borneo- Mekong - Southeast Asia Basin - The region is composed of two sub-regions, the द्वीप [Island] of Borneo and the Mekong River.
  • Maximum plant species are found in the evergreen rainforests. Hardwood trees like – Rosewood, Aabnoos, Mahogany etc. are found in these forests. • Tropical पर्णपाती वन [Deciduous Forest] – They are also known as monsoonal forests. They are mostly found in India, northern Australia, Indonesia the Central America. There are two distinctive seasons (moist and dry) in these forests. India and its nearby regions experience three distinctive seasons thus even the forests of the area experience seasonal changes. The main flora of these forests includes Sal, Teak, Neem and Rosewood. Apart from these other trees, herbs and shrubs are also found. • Temperate Evergreen Forests – They are found in the coastal areas of mid-latitudes. They are normally found on the eastern sides of the continent such as south east USA, South China and southeastern Brazil. Temperate evergreen forests are dominated by specific species of trees. The trees have broad leaves and hardwood for example – Oak, Laurel, Magnolia, and Eucalyptus etc. • Mediterranean Forest – These forests are found in the mid-latitudes on the western and southwestern parts of the continent. They are located in regions receiving winter rainfall. Mediterranean forests are mostly found in the regions of Africa, Europe and Asia that lie in proximity to the Mediterranean Sea. These forests are also found in California and southwestern Australia. The main trees of the region are Cork, Oak, Olives, Plums, Chestnut, Pine etc. The Mediterranean region is famous for the production of Citrus fruits such as Grapes, Lemons, Oranges, Olives, Pears, Pomegranate, etc. •Temperate Coniferous Forests – Also known as टैगा वन [Taiga / Boreal Forest] Forest. They are found between 50 o to 60o North latitude. To their north they have tundra and to their south, they have deciduous forests. They are found in regions of North America, Eurasia and sub-polar regions. They are found in Canada, Europe, Asia and the USA. Temperate coniferous forests cover the largest area in the world. Important flora of the region includes – Deodar, Fir, Hemlock, Spruce, etc. In India, these forests are found in high Himalayan regions. •According to data from the World Food and Agriculture Statistical Yearbook, 2025 (Data - 2023). U.S.A. is the leading producer of wood pulp in the world (47982 million tonnes) followed by Brazil and China.

This statement is associated with : (a) Thornthwaite (b) Koppen (c) T rewartha (d) Stamp U.P. P.C.S. (Pre) 2018 Ans. (b) German-Russian botanist and climatologist Vladimir Koppen presented a descriptive classification of the world's climate in the year 1884, which was revised and refined in the years 1918, 1931 and 1936. Koppen has considered vegetation as the true indicator of climate. This has made the five vegetation zones of the world proposed by Candoll in 1874 AD the basis of the division of the world's climate. Later, like Koppen, Thornthwaite also propounded that vegetation is an indicator of climate and that the amount of rainfall and temperature has a substantial effect on vegetation, but he took evaporation into account. On this basis, Thornthwaite has given importance to precipitation effectiveness in demarcation of climate. Thornthwaite has accepted vegetation as an indirect element of climate. 46. The climate is temperate with precipitation of 600 to 1200 mm. The climate is generally temperate, gradually giving way to a more subtropical climate in the north and to a semi-arid climate on the western fringes. The Soil ∗Soils are formed as a result of disintegration and decomposition of loosened and unorganized rock materials, fossils of flora and fauna, water and air. Bedrock, climatic and biotic factors play a vital role in the formation of soil. The color, formation and minerals of the soil depend upon the bedrock.
